===PAGE 2=== (plain, 29 lines)
   1| 2
   2| In Memoriam
   3|  Lillian S. Griffiths
   4| Lillian passed away on September 30th at the age of 99. Lillian grew up on the family farm in
   5| Lunenburg with four brothers and five sisters. After distinguishing herself as a scholar-athlete at
   6| Lunenburg High and graduating from Fitchburg State in 1941, she began her 33-year career in
   7| education as a junior high teacher in Lunenburg.  She went on to teach fifth grade, earn her
   8| Master’s Degree from Boston University. and serve as the Reading Supervisor for grades K-8
   9| from 1957 until her retirement in 1977. She was past president of the Lunenburg Teachers’
  10| Association and the North Worcester County’s Council on Reading.  She was an avid golfer and
  11| past president of the Oak Hill Country Club Women’s Golf Association. She also coached
  12| basketball and field hockey at Lunenburg High and was deeply honored to be one of the first ten
  13| inductees to the LHS Athletic Hall of Fame in 2007.
  14| Walter Barrett Keeler, age 90, died April 24th.  Mr. Keeler was active in the
  15| Lunenburg community, taking leadership positions in the United Parish; serving on the School
  16| Committee from 1965-1974; helping to found Hickory Hills Landowners Inc. in 1978 which
  17| purchased the lake for residents and shareholders; working tirelessly to have Hickory Hills roads
  18| taken over by the Town; and culminating in service on the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen from
  19| 1984-1987.
  20| Sarah Powell, 103, passed away on September 13, 2017. She was the recipient of the Boston
  21| Post Cane Award in 2015. At that time, she said she was ancient. Sarah’s wit will be missed by
  22| all, as well as her special ability to say the alphabet backwards!
  23| Einard Warpula, 89, passed away on May 17th at home surrounded by family. Einard lived on
  24| the same property on Flat Hill Road in Lunenburg for 87 years. Einard owned Warpula Bus
  25| Company for 25 years, safely transporting thousands of students in Lunenburg. Upon  "retiring,"
  26| he drove motor-coaches for Wilson Bus Lines and school buses for local contractors, right up
  27| until last December. He was a wealth of knowledge on best-routes, landmarks, parking spots and
  28| all things mechanical. Einard loved to drive and trained many others to drive as well. He loved a
  29| good story and a good laugh and always found something positive in every situation.

===PAGE 5=== (plain, 36 lines)
   1| 5
   2| LUNENBURG PROFILE
   3| YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728
   4| FY’17 TAX RATE-$19.98
   5| OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   6| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   7| ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   8| AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
   9| ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
  10| Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It  occupies a  land area of
  11| approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
  12| approximately 43 miles from Boston.  It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
  13| City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancas ter and the City of Leominster, on the east
  14| by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
  15| Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting o f all registered voters
  16| in the t own. Subject to the legislative decisions  made by town meeti ng, the affairs of the t own
  17| are generally administered by a five member Board of S electmen and a  Town Manager. Local
  18| school affairs are administered by an elected seven member School Committee while local taxes
  19| are assessed by a three me mber Board of A ssessors, all are appointed on an at -large basis for
  20| staggered three-year terms.
  21| Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Three water bodies
  22| account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the t own's histori cal development.  Lake
  23| Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the
  24| remainder of Lunenburg.  This district was developed as a housing base for  many factory
  25| workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg.  In many ways the
  26| characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these cities than of
  27| the rest of Lunenb urg. The Hickory Hills area of t own, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  28| developed as a private community comprise d largely of vacation cottages.  The third body of
  29| water is Shirley Re servoir, a public water body also developed with mainly vacation cottages
  30| that have now been modified to year -round residences. Commercial and industrial development
  31| is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major arteries (Rout es 2A and 13),
  32| and the t own's peripheries , due largely to the close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's
  33| sewer and water systems.  Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into  single
  34| family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg.  At the Annual Town Meeting on May 4 th,
  35| 2013, the Town voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve, and
  36| enhance farming in the community and to administer the “Right to Farm” bylaw.

===PAGE 16=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 16
   2| REPORT OF THE
   3| TOWN MANAGER
   4| The calendar year of 2017 was another year of progress for the Board of Selectmen and Town
   5| Manager’s Office.  During 2017 there were many topics addressed and accomplishments to
   6| highlight:
   7|  On January 17th, the new Town Manager, Heather Lemieux, began.  During this month
   8| the Town Manager pursued entering the Commonwealth’s Community Compact Program
   9| to implement up to three best practices.  By entering the program, the State would
  10| provide grant funds to implement these best practices and it would also enable the Town
  11| to be eligible for an Information Technology Grant and a Regionalization/Efficiency
  12| Grant.  At the Board’s first meeting in February the Selectmen chose three best practices,
  13| including to “Implement a Citizen Engagement Co-Creation Plan”, to “Implement
  14| Stormwater Management Measures”, and to “Implement a Cyber-Security Training
  15| Program”.  The Town entered the program in February and a ceremony was held with
  16| Lieutenant Governor Karyn Polito on May 4th to sign the compact.  All three best
  17| practices are expected to be completed by June of 2018.
  18|  Other noteworthy items that happened in February include a Roads Workshop that was
  19| held to review the list of roads that would be paved for the ensuing year as part of the
  20| $4M debt exclusion for a pavement management plan, as well as a discussion on entering
  21| the Complete Streets Program under Massachusetts Department of Transportation that
  22| would improve accessibility and safety of travel modes for all ages and abilities.  Town
  23| Manager Heather Lemieux, Selectmen Chairman Jamie Toale, Land Use Director Adam
  24| Burney and DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz formulated a Complete Streets policy that
  25| was approved by the Board in April of 2017.  The policy was submitted to MassDOT and
  26| received a favorable scoring that enabled the Town to receive $37,000 in grant funds to
  27| hire a consultant to assist the Town to put together a list of projects into a Prioritization
  28| Plan.  Once completed this would be submitted to MassDOT and the Town would be
  29| eligible to receive up to $400,000 in construction funds to complete projects on the list.
  30| It is estimated the Prioritization Plan will be completed by May of 2018.  Also in
  31| February the Selectmen interviewed nine law firms and in April selected Mead, Talerman
  32| & Costa as the Town’s new Town Counsel, who took over from KP Law in July.  The
  33| Town Manager presented a balanced budget for Fiscal Year 2018 to the Finance
  34| Committee and Selectmen in mid-February for a total of $39,781,168.  Part of the Town
  35| Manager’s recommendation was to begin to fund the Other Post-Employment Benefits
  36| Trust Fund for the unfunded liability of retiree health insurance in the amount of
  37| $100,000 and to create a Special Purpose Stabilization Account for known large capital
  38| items such as fire trucks with a starting appropriation of $200,000.

===PAGE 17=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 17
   2|  During the month of March the Town received notification that the Regionalization and
   3| Efficiency Grant application for a feasibility and architectural design of a Regional
   4| Animal Control facility was awarded to Lunenburg, Fitchburg, and Townsend in the
   5| amount of $42,257.  Fitchburg later backed out due to privatization of their animal
   6| control services, leaving Lunenburg as the lead community. The expected completion of
   7| a design is by June 2018.
   8|  The Town received an Indoor Air Quality Report for Town Hall based on testing done by
   9| the Department of Public Health in the fall of 2016.  There were a number of
  10| recommendations made including remediation of the mold in the Town Hall basement,
  11| seal the basement to eliminate any future moisture problems, clean bird waste in the attic,
  12| and clean the HVAC system.  A portion of these were accomplished during FY2017
  13| using funds from the Town Facilities account and a Reserve Fund Transfer authorized by
  14| the Finance Committee.  The remainder of the funding was appropriated at the Special
  15| Town Meeting on November 28th and all projects are expected to be complete by the end
  16| of Fiscal Year 2018.
  17|  The Summer Street redevelopment and roadway improvement project continued to move
  18| forward with a slated beginning of 2018.  The estimated $10M costs for this project
  19| which includes Lunenburg, Leominster and Fitchburg, will be funded through the
  20| Regional Transportation Improvement Plan (TIP), which are state and federal
  21| transportation funds.
  22|  The Right of Way process was finalized by the Town in August and met the deadline
  23| MassDOT’s deadline to advertise the project at the end of August.
  24|  In May the Selectmen approved an Entertainment License at 300 Holman Street after
  25| receiving input from all relevant public safety and public health departments, as well as
  26| residents at the public hearing.  The Annual Town Meeting on May 6th passed a balanced
  27| budget for FY 2018 with an omnibus budget of $35,690,393.70.  Amendments to the FY
  28| 2018 budget were made at a Special Town Meeting in November for a revised total of
  29| $35,855,365.70.
  30|  The Board held a Tax Title Workshop in May to review the list of properties provided by
  31| the Treasurer/Collector and discuss properties that the Conservation Commission and
  32| Selectmen were interested in foreclosing as they abutted existing conservation land.  The
  33| Selectmen agreed on a finalized list of properties to proceed with a Tax Title Assignment
  34| Auction and the remainder of the properties to proceed through the foreclosure process.
  35|  The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY
  36| 2018.  The Selectmen’s workshop in June was to review the Traffic Rules & Regulations
  37| with Counsel Joel Bard and Police Chief James Marino which became finalized in
  38| September 2017.

===PAGE 18=== (plain, 40 lines)
   1| 18
   2|  In June the Town was notified that the Information Technology grant the Town Manager
   3| submitted was awarded in the amount of $40,000 to purchase electronic permitting
   4| software utilized by various town departments. After a selection process, the Town chose
   5| Viewpoint.  The e-permitting software will create greater efficiencies both internally and
   6| externally for users that apply for various permits and licenses as they will be able to log
   7| in remotely, conduct business, and check the status of their application.  The software is
   8| expected to go “live” for external users in late May 2018.
   9|  In July the Town Manager presented various updates to the Board’s Policies and
  10| Procedures Manual including personnel policies, a new social media policy and revised
  11| financial policies recommended by the Finance Committee.  Also in July, the Selectmen
  12| made appointments to the Board of Health and Planning Board to fill vacancies until the
  13| next annual election and re-appointed the Animal Control Officer.
  14|  The development of a plan for town facilities, specifically the Town Hall, the Ritter
  15| Building, the old Primary, the Brooks House, and the Passios continued to be a consistent
  16| topic at many Selectmen meetings, the Annual Town Meeting and the Special Town
  17| Meeting.  In August the Board held a workshop to discuss town facilities, their current
  18| and future use. At that meeting the Board voted to move forward with the concept of
  19| consolidating town offices under one roof at the Passios Building.  A public forum was
  20| held on December 7th to receive input from the public on the current and future use of
  21| town facilities.
  22|  The Town auctioned off five properties that were town owned due to foreclosure on
  23| August 29th receiving a total of $37,000 that will positively impact the Town’s FY19 Free
  24| Cash.  The Town Manager presented a change to the Pay-As-You Throw program that
  25| included changing to a Retail Store Distribution model where the bag vendor delivers the
  26| bags directly to the stores that distribute them, added an Extra Large bag, increased the
  27| thickness of the Large bag, and changed from a twist tie to drawstring bag.
  28|  A Five Year Moratorium for Road Openings policy presented by the DPW Director was
  29| adopted by the Board in September with an associated fee schedule that was approved in
  30| November.  The September workshop was a joint meeting with the Planning Board on
  31| Economic Development to discuss the work that has been done by the Town in the past
  32| on economic development, types and level of economic development and next steps.  The
  33| Selectmen held another economic development workshop in December to include the
  34| Sewer Commission, Planning Board, and the North Central Chamber of Commerce. The
  35| Town Manager applied for and received District Local Technical Assistance that
  36| provided consultant services from Montachusett Regional Planning Commission to
  37| facilitate meetings between the Town and business community that would focus on
  38| promoting the local economy.  There were a total of three meetings with residents and
  39| business owners and MRPC presented their written report with recommendations at a
  40| Selectmen’s meeting in December.

===PAGE 19=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 19
   2|  After conducting an online survey to solicit the input on the color of Town Hall (the current
   3| brown color or white), over 600 respondents voted, with a majority choosing white.  The project
   4| was put out to bid in September and bids were received in October.  The total cost for the project
   5| came in at budget of $88,000 using funds that were approved through a previous town meeting
   6| capital planning article.  The Town Manager announced the award of a Department of Energy
   7| Resources Technical Assistance grant in the amount of $5,000 for grant administration services
   8| for the Green Communities to prepare a competitive grant application to be submitted in March
   9| of 2018.
  10|  The Board conducted a series of liquor license violation hearings in October, a workshop with
  11| town boards/committees/commissions to review accomplishments and goals, as well as how the
  12| Selectmen could support them.
  13|  The Special Town Meeting on November 28th included a number of articles such as amending the
  14| FY 2018 budget to capture additional tax growth in both real estate and personal property, a
  15| citizen’s petition to supplement funding approved at previous town meeting to improve Wallis
  16| Park, additional revolving fund accounts, fund the first year of the Firefighter and Clerical Union
  17| Agreements, funding to perform property condition assessments for 6 town and 1 school building,
  18| funding for a conceptual design for town offices at the Passios building, acceptance of certain
  19| Massachusetts General Laws, authorization for a telecommunications lease at Town Hall for up to
  20| 25 years, acceptance of land for conservation at 842R Chase Road, and an amendment to the
  21| Zoning Bylaw.
  22|  The Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler, weekday entertainment,
  23| automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.
  24| After just over a year in the position, Lunenburg has become my home away from home. The residents of
  25| Lunenburg demonstrate the true meaning of community that I have witnessed in various acts, whether it
  26| be supporting our local businesses, non-profit groups raising funds for those in need, or words of
  27| gratitude for our public safety and public works departments.  I have the privilege of working with many
  28| talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents and business owners of Lunenburg and are
  29| consistently looking for ways to better serve the public.  The accomplishments noted above, among the
  30| numerous day-to-day responsibilities, could not happen without the cooperation of dedicated staff,
  31| appointed, and elected officials that make the wheels of town government turn.  Thank you to the Board
  32| of Selectmen for the level of respect that each of you gives your fellow members, the staff, and the public
  33| and well thought out decisions.  Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson, for all your hard
  34| work and keeping the office running smoothly.  I would also like to thank the committee and board
  35| members, department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  36| Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the department heads that lead our various departments:
  37| James Marino, Police Chief; Patrick Sullivan, Fire Chief; Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director; Karen
  38| Brochu, Town Accountant/Finance Director; Myleen Mallari, Treasurer/Collector; Louise Paquette,
  39| Assessors; Steve Malandrinos, IT Director; Adam Burney, Land Use Director; Sue Doherty, COA
  40| Director; Martha Moore, Library Director; Kathy Herrick, Town Clerk;  and all their staff for their talent
  41| and working together to make our town run as efficiently as possible.  Lastly, thank you to the residents
  42| and businesses that make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.
  43| Respectfully submitted,
  44| Heather R. Lemieux

   2| ARCHITECTURAL PRESERVATION  DISTRICT COMMISSION
   3| (APDC)
   4| In 2017, the Lunenburg APDC held regular monthly meetings, as well as public hearings:
   5| both on its own and in conjunction with the Board of Selectmen.  The main focus of work
   6| was to draft guidelines for implementation of the Architectural Preservation District bylaw
   7| and amend them so that they were acceptable to the Board of Selectmen.  In November, after
   8| a vote of approval from the Board of Selectmen, the APDC voted to adopt the amended
   9| guidelines and submitted then to the Town Clerk, making the Architectural Preservation
  10| District bylaw fully operational.
  11|         BUILDING DEPARTMENT
  12| The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massa-
  13| chusetts Avenue.  Office hours for the Building Department are Monday and Wednesday 8:00 A.M.
  14| to 4:00 P.M., Tuesday and Thursday 8:00 A.M. until 6:00 P.M.  The Department is closed on Friday.
  15| Garry Rhodes serves as Building Commissioner, Andrew Hudson serves as the Assistant Building
  16| Inspector and Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Building Department.
  17| Lisa Normandin also serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board of Appeals.
  18| Building Permits for 2017 have increased from 500 in 2016 to 538.  Solar permits continue to be
  19| strong.  We continue to see permits for weatherization which can be contributed to support provided
  20| by “Mass Save.”  In many cases Mass Save provides financial support to homeowners who upgrade
  21| insulation and do air sealing.
  22| The state of Massachusetts, as of January 1 st 2018, has implemented the 9 th Edition of the Building
  23| Code, CMR 780.  The changes will impact both residential and commercial construction.  The ninth
  24| edition is based on 2015 International Building Code.
  25| We receive many inquiries from residents concerning sheds.  You only need a building permit when
  26| the shed is over 200 square feet.  Even though a building permit may not be required the shed must
  27| comply with Lunenburg Zoning Bylaw for location.  The side and rear yard setback varies from 5
  28| feet to 15 feet depending on the zoning district.  You may also be subject to setbacks for both Con-
  29| servation (wetlands) and Board of Health (septic systems).
  30| Building Permits have been issued for all 120 apartment units known as Whalom Luxury Apartments
  31| located off Whalom Road.  Occupancy permits have been issued for the first building.
  32| The forms to apply for permits can be obtained on -line at www.lunenburgma.gov or by stopping by
  33| at 960 Massachusetts Avenue during normal business hours.

   2| CEMETERY COMMISSION
   3| The Cemetery Commission had a busy 2017, as the installation of the Niche Wall was com-
   4| pleted in the North Cemetery, while paving and tree pruning was completed in the South
   5| Cemetery. The Lunenburg Cemetery Commission continues to plan for the future, as the
   6| Commission oversaw an additional 20 services that included burials and cremations.
   7| In planning for future growth, the Cemetery Commission has begun to do so, with the addi-
   8| tion of a completed Niche Wall that adds to the availability of planned space and offers a
   9| modern and updated way for families to make additional choices for their loved ones.
  10| Lunenburg Boy Scout Troop 1728, through their Scoutmaster Jim Parker, Scout Michael
  11| Clark and his father Mike Clark, together with other Scouts and their parents, completed the
  12| North Cemetery Flag Pole Eagle Scout Project and did a great job!

===PAGE 51=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 51
   2|                                                                        CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   3| The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven member commission, appointed to 3 year terms by
   4| the Board of Selectmen, whose duties are codified under the Conservation Commission Act MGL chapter 40 s 8C.
   5| The Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL chapter 131 s 40 and Article XXII of
   6| the local bylaws for the protection of wetlands.
   7|  The year 2017 was, as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and
   8| meetings for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family up-
   9| keep projects and a large scale solar proposal. The Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of prepar-
  10| ing to use the year 2018 to complete revising its regulations to provide consistency with the original by -law and
  11| to find those sections that may be now out of date and need to be modified. This effort has been methodically
  12| approached and very comprehensive and is estimated to be completed by summer of 2018.
  13| The Commission also held numerous inspections with the commission administrator on weekends prior to
  14| meetings to familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The Com-
  15| mission continues to work with the town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates from
  16| the EPA and working towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codification.
  17| The Conservation Commission completed forest stewardship planning after receiving grants (see table on page
  18| 2). These stewardship plans for five individual parcels, will aid the commission in implementing environmental
  19| management plans which include timber stand management. This December, the Commission bid and initiated
  20| timber and environmental management in the small town forest. Other Conservation areas plans have been
  21| completed for are the Lane property, The Large and Small Town Forests, the open space on Robbs Hill and the
  22| Cowdery. The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup, a noted forester with many years of experience working in
  23| town for the commission and private landowners. The Conservation Commission also held public recreational
  24| walks through its open space parcels and plans to continue to do so.
  25| The Commission has also worked closely with Lake Shirley and Hickory Hills in the implementations of their man-
  26| agement plans becoming actively involved in continuation of formulating updates designed to give a better pic-
  27| ture of the overall health of the ecosystems and the various ways to ensure continued protection of these valua-
  28| ble resources.
  29| This year also marked the departure of Commissioner Thomas Bertram. Tom continues to assist the Commission
  30| in the matters of trails and open space management. We wish Tom well in future endeavors and are grateful for
  31| both his previous service and his willingness to continue to contribute! The Commission would like to take this
  32| time to welcome Catie Childs as our newest commissioner. Catie, a former member of a Conservation Commis-
  33| sion in another community she previously resided, presented the Commission with a qualified candidate who
  34| could start off the ground running. We welcome and value her presence as the newest member of the Lunen-
  35| burg Conservation Commission!
  36| The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and look forward to many more!
  37| Respectfully Submitted,
  38| Richard Bursch, Chair
  39| Todd Dwyer, Vice-Chair
  40| Bob Pease
  41| Catie Childs
  42| Carl Luck
  43| Kenneth Jones
  44| Jack Rabbitt
  45| Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator

   1| 53
   2|                                                      COUNCIL ON AGING
   3| The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   4| supportive services, programs and education.  Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts Gen-
   5| eral Law Chapter 40, § 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or services and to
   6| develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   7| The Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative Assistant
   8| and Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach Coordinator; Joanne Brazell, Meal Site Coordinator;  Judy
   9| Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants;  Jim McGuigan and Paul Grunditz, MART Van Drivers.
  10| The Council on Aging Board is an 11 member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Board of Selectmen.
  11| The COA Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln,  Vice-Chairperson Jacquelyn Dwyer, Betty DiGia-
  12| como,Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally, Cheryl Moisan, Diane Nowd, Jane Rabbitt,
  13| and Judith Tarbell.
  14| The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House Sup-
  15| porters Inc., a non-profit 501(c)3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts
  16| and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority. We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle
  17| House.  In FY17 we had 41 volunteers who contributed 2,218.5 hours of their time to help us doing things
  18| such as driving for the Meals on Wheels Program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, office help, library, and
  19| friendly visitors.  We greatly appreciate their service.
  20| In July of FY17, the Eagle House Senior Community Center re-opened again on Fridays for business.  This
  21| paved the way for more programming and transportation opportunities for our residents.
  22| The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts, intergenerational,
  23| bocce, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, and social events both at the senior center and
  24| offsite.  In FY17 we offered 21 new programs which included various speakers and exercise programs.
  25| There were approximately 906 people who utilized the senior center in FY17.  Those 906 people compiled
  26| 25,986 units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
  27| In FY17 the Council on Aging delivered 4,183 Meals on Wheels to 48 clients and provided 3,117 rides to 64
  28| clients on our senior van.
  29| We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
  30| supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders. Special thanks go to our 501(c)3
  31| fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. who donated $9,214 for equipment and programs at the Eagle
  32| House Senior Community Center.  Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for donating time and
  33| programming for our clients.
  34| The Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you to pick up at the Eagle House
  35| Senior Community Center, Town Hall, Library, Post Office, local churches and various businesses throughout
  36| the community.

===PAGE 55=== (plain, 46 lines)
   1| 55
   2| DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   3| The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and
   4| Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel
   5| and equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope
   6| of services with a high degree of efficiency.
   7| DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
   8| “Public Works is either working or on call twenty -four hours a day three hundred and sixty -five days a
   9| year to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise.”
  10| We strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure oth-
  11| ers are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not
  12| forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of service.
  13| Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have highlighted the fact
  14| that Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency management. Town Officials, Police,
  15| and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must
  16| continue to build on this awareness.
  17| This department must also reach out to the public we serve and educate them to the fact that the common
  18| services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals, service com-
  19| panies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling operations, tree re-
  20| moval, storm water management facilities and grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries
  21| and parks are the expected services supporting everyone in the community.
  22| We will continue to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for an-
  23| nual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  24| PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  25| Historically the amount of funding dedicated to roadway paving and paving related services has been
  26| grossly inadequate resulting in a negative pavement condition index. It became a high priority goal of the BOS as
  27| Road Commissioners, to achieve a pavement condition of 72% -75% Town-wide. Although State funded Chapter
  28| 90 funds remained stagnant, the dedicated Pavement Management line in the operational budget grew substantial-
  29| ly.
  30| Results of the newly revised Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan displayed clearly the inequity
  31| of the distribution of paving funds toward higher use main and collector roads leaving local neighborhoods unat-
  32| tended. Using the PMP as evidence, residents went to the polls to vote favorably on a four -million-dollar debt
  33| exclusion targeting exclusively the local roads. Expending approximately a million dollars in the initial year re-
  34| sulted in a major enhancement of road conditions.
  35| The debt exclusion money combined with state and operational funds brought the total paving value to
  36| nearly two million dollars in the first construction season as we attacked the nine -million-dollar backlog of re-
  37| pairs.
  38| Our pothole log has and is expected to lessen exponentially as new paving increases, a clear benefit of
  39| the dollars expended. There is much work yet to do since we are at the very beginning of the program but expec-
  40| tations are high.
  41| The seasonal crack sealing program has been effective in some locations where applicable however this
  42| protocol is designed as a temporary solution with an effective life span of around five years.  The mechanics of
  43| the process involves the installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure into cracks or separations in the
  44| pavement. The Pavement Management Plan identifies specific areas where this method is the best option.  This
  45| application does not take the place of curb to curb paving, only postpones it and is not applicable in areas of more
  46| serious defects.

===PAGE 56=== (layout, 44 lines)
   1| FLEET MAINTENANCE
   2|            The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance to all Town vehicles and equip-
   3| ment. High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park, cemetery, and school vehi-
   4| cles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by department personnel. Outsourcing of
   5| highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore costlier. Due to the volatile global oil market, the
   6| overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products continues to be a moving target and therefore difficult to esti-
   7| mate annually.
   8|            The installation of a vehicle maintenance software program continues to be a valuable tool. Each repair is
   9| logged by department and vehicle identification number and memorialized in a data bank. Every vehicle can be
  10| tracked for part numbers, required services, as well as frequency and cost of repairs over the life of the equipment.
  11| As Director I have created and submitted to Town Officials a vehicle replacement schedule based upon that creat-
  12| ed by the American Public Works Association in order to more clearly represent our capital needs.
  13| FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  14| The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  15|                  DPW Garage 520 Chase Road                        Public Safety Complex
  16|                  Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                        Town Hall
  17|                  Cemetery Bldg. Holman St.                        Eagle House
  18|                  Town Beach                                       Town Common
  19|                  Ritter Bldg.                                     Teen Center
  20|                  Old Primary School                                 Veterans Park
  21|                  All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
  22|         A facilities staff of two individuals remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year.
  23| Staff attempts to keep buildings compliant with current codes to insure a safe secure work environment. Any sig-
  24| nificant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of disrupting or disabling necessary
  25| services provided to the community. The facilities support staff are problem solvers and technicians with a wide
  26| range of experience encompassing all of the construction trades. They deliver services or oversee those functions
  27| provided by specialized subcontractors. Staff manages and forecasts all of the building assets and prioritizes re-
  28| placement costs. Unfortunately, this component of the capital program has fallen short of expectations. Years of
  29| neglect have created a significant backlog of necessary repairs and improvements. The process has become reac-
  30| tionary at best. The newly formed Building Reuse Committee reported process has become reactionary at best.
  31| The Building Reuse Committee reported clearly that asset management relative to our buildings requires a profes-
  32| sionally prepared assessment and a 5-year capital plan. Public Works continues its use of a building maintenance
  33| software program as a framework for day to day activities. Requests for service are acknowledged immediately,
  34| prioritized, and completed in a timely manner. Those individuals requesting service are contacted upon comple-
  35| tion of the tasks and a history of the repair is filed electronically.
  36| ENVIRONMENTAL
  37| Storm Water
  38|            As a component of the aforementioned “debt exclusion” approximately five hundred thousand dollars of
  39| the four million in total has been earmarked for storm drainage including but not limited to repair of the existing
  40| collection system, and additional construction.
  41|            As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed to
  42| avoid paving over problems. New Federal and State storm water regulations will likely force the Town to become
  43| more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of funding.
  44|                                                                     56

===PAGE 57=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 57
   2|  At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall drainage structures in close proximity to open wa-
   3| ter are cleaned and inspected. Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street sweeping and catch basin
   4| cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
   5|  Waste Water
   6| The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. Day to day maintenance of the overall
   7| wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations is outsourced to a specialty vendor but continues to be the re-
   8| sponsibility of Public Works. Business management, inspections, and permitting, remain in -house and the re-
   9| sponsibility of the DPW.
  10| Tree Removal
  11|  Storm damage together with the natural attrition of every species creates a backlog of tree felling and
  12| pruning that far exceeds the allotted budget. The department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must
  13| prioritize work based upon the calculated degree of danger to residents and property. With the establishment of a
  14| Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established through prioritization. Sadly,
  15| the amount of work far exceeds the available revenue. Until there is a substantial infusion of funds, the DPW will
  16| act in a reactionary mode. All work aside from brush removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is
  17| outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff
  18| initiates the activity of removing and disposing of woody debris within the right -of-way together with a Town -
  19| wide program of curbside mowing. A aggressive program by our energy provider has lessened the burden of the
  20| Town to remove those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  21| Landfill
  22| The “capped” landfill off Youngs Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated
  23| by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as well
  24| as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  25| The frequency of the testing, reduced through a special permit, acquired as a result of consistently fa-
  26| vorable results, remained constant throughout the year. The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is in-
  27| spected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  28| Adjacent to the “cap” is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined
  29| by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a
  30| (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials. Other commu-
  31| nities are required to transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at an excessive cost.
  32| On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual
  33| composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and add a val-
  34| uable component to our recycling program. There has to date been no dedicated funding toward the organization
  35| or long range remediation of this location. Once again, dollars will be taken from the General Highway line to
  36| maintain this location while keeping it organized and environmentally safe. It is likely this department will pre-
  37| sent a plan to use the compost as leverage to clean and grade the area as well as the removal of the brush. With
  38| water and sewer at the perimeter this sandy, gravelly area adjacent has been proposed as a potential recreational
  39| area for the Town. As of yet this project has not gained traction.
  40| Solid Waste
  41| Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collec-
  42| tion of trash and recyclables by our current vendor, Casella Inc. Pay as you throw…
  43| Lake Shirley Dam
  44|         In the early 1990s, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned over
  45| ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance,
  46| monitoring, and record keeping was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Associa-
  47| tion. In 2004 -2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the establishment of an
  48| operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of the Department of Pub-
  49| lic Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance with current and future

===PAGE 58=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 58
   2| future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional consultant to assist with
   3| predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report.
   4| Winter Operations
   5| Snow and ice present a major challenge to our Town. The safety of the general public depends on the
   6| ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe condition despite the severity or the duration of snow and
   7| ice events. To that end we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan that must
   8| be flexible due to the unpredictability of each storm. A consistently high level of service is expected and must
   9| be delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
  10| Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.  Financial sup-
  11| port is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of services due to breakdowns
  12| and repairs.  In the fall, salt and sand are delivered to the Chase Rd. facility. Lunenburg is in a consortium with
  13| 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price for road salt, an absolute necessity. From
  14| late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, spreaders and plows for
  15| winter operations. During the construction season road crews address right -of-way issues, overhanging branch-
  16| es, and pavement defects in order to eliminate obstacles to snow removal. Each division within the DPW is in-
  17| volved in winter operations. Typically, DPW staff combines with 18-20 subcontractors to provide service.
  18| Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall cost.
  19| For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6 -8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freezing
  20| rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the department operates in a deficit due to a
  21| yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter.
  22|  It is a dedicated group of individuals who successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer regard-
  23| less of severity or duration.
  24| “DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers”
  25| PARKS
  26| In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks
  27| and grounds exclusive of school property. While resources of the DPW are available as required, much of the
  28| Town-wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, resulting in significant savings and increased efficiency over
  29| an in-house operation. Repair of the multi -use track complex is imminent. After many years, a mutual effort
  30| between the Commissioners and all users of the facility is gaining traction.
  31| CEMETERY
  32| One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South
  33| cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of the
  34| Cemetery Commission.  The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year
  35| and general maintenance of the facilities.
  36| As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow
  37| Town employees as we persevere.
  38| As caretakers of the Town’s infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the
  39| Town of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated
  40| with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works.
  41| Respectfully Submitted
  42|             Jack Rodriquenz
  43|           Director

===PAGE 59=== (layout, 35 lines)
   1|                                 FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   2|   (This is a reprint of the message printed in the May 6, 2017 Annual Town Meeting Warrant.)
   3| The Town Budget to be presented for approval is much the same as it has been in recent
   4| years.  Our Department Heads were asked to send a ‘level service’ budget, and requests be-
   5| yond level service, to the new Town Manager.  Based on the financial forecast and funding
   6| opportunities, she is proposing increases beyond level service in some high need areas, such
   7| as police and fire, as well as an increase in the schools budget that is funded largely by an
   8| increase in Chapter 70 funds from the state.  The budget anticipates an increase in revenues
   9| from all sources, totaling $1,701,629.  Half of this increase is anticipated in state aid through
  10| Chapter 70 (education funding) and general government (lottery aid).    While this is wel-
  11| come news, unrestricted government aid still lags behind earlier levels.  Health insurance
  12| increases are projected to be less severe than last year.
  13| For every $1,000 in revenue to the Town, $643 comes from real estate property taxes. $357
  14| comes from other sources such as state aid, motor vehicle or meal taxes, betterments, and
  15| other fees.  This represents a decrease in reliance on property taxes of about 2.6%.  A large
  16| portion of this is due to a 13.9% increase in Chapter 70 funds.
  17| Another, more tentative source is Free Cash. The effects of state aid on particular aspects of
  18| the budget are difficult to parse out, except for Chapter 70 funding, which accounts for $1.21
  19| of every $3.00 budgeted for the schools.   The School Committee put forward an increase of
  20| $1,266,606, which is heavily reliant on the Governor’s Chapter 70 spending recommenda-
  21| tions making it through the legislature.  The School Department has the greatest budget
  22| share at 53.4 percent.  As reported, property tax funding accounts for about 60 percent of
  23| this budget.
  24| Because of the positive growth, and lack of new spending requirements, the Town is now in
  25| the position to address some ongoing concerns.  This budget adds services to Public Safety
  26| through 1 Police Officer and 1 Firefighter.  Additionally, our Regional Dispatch assessment
  27| through the Radio Watch portion of the budget was decreased sufficiently to offset increased
  28| weekend coverage at the Public Safety Building.  Last year, the Town committed to increas-
  29| ing the pavement management plan by $50,000 per year, funded from increases in motor
  30| vehicle excise taxes, to ensure that recent improvements paid for through debt exclusion
  31| would be adequately maintained going forward.  $50,000 of the DPW budget increase ad-
  32| dresses this.   The Council on Aging is increased $22,080, moving all costs for the Outreach
  33| Worker to the Town budget.  Historically, part of this position has been covered with grant
  34| funds.  Most of the rest of the departmental increases address contractual salary obligations.
  35|                                                           59

===PAGE 60=== (plain, 33 lines)
   1| 60
   2| FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT
   3| (This is a reprint of the message printed in the May 6, 2017 Annual Town Meeting Warrant.)
   4| Last year, we noted a trend of Debt Service, Retirement, and Health Insurance taking up
   5| larger portions of the budget, squeezing out other operational needs.  This year the trend
   6| seems to have hit a pause as increases in Retirement and Insurance has stabilized, and Debt
   7| Service has actually decreased by $61,157.   The Town for the first time is funding the
   8| OPEB Stabilization Fund ($100,000) for future liability for post-employment employee
   9| benefits, other than pensions, which has been an issue that the Town has discussed for some
  10| time.  We continue to weigh the long term effect of adding new positions against the cur-
  11| rent needs and opportunities the town faces.
  12| The Retirement Assessment has gone up 5 percent this year, compared to 9 percent last
  13| year.  Town employees pay into 1 of 2 retirement systems.  Licensed school district em-
  14| ployees contribute to the Massachusetts Teacher Retirement System, which requires no ad-
  15| ditional town funding.  Any additional funding of this Retirement System is done at the
  16| State level, and is reported in notes to the Town Financial Statements as an ‘On Behalf’
  17| payment.  Non -licensed school personnel and Town side employees contribute to the
  18| Worcester County Retirement System.  The Town makes two payments to this system, an
  19| employer contribution for active employees and an assessment to fund the ‘Unfunded Lia-
  20| bility’ of future payments to retirees.
  21| The Capital Planning Committee has recommended funding 10 items at $633,317.  This
  22| budget funds the 10 items.  There is no request, nor recommendation to borrow funds to
  23| fund any other items. In anticipation of some large capital requests in the coming years (i.e.
  24| Fire Trucks), there is a request to form a Capital Stabilization Fund so the Town can set
  25| aside funds in a dedicated account to lower the amounts the Town may have to borrow.
  26| In summary, the budget presented addresses at least current service levels, with some in-
  27| crease in some departments.  The Finance Committee will continue to address the question
  28| of ensuring the budget addresses the priorities of the Town of Lunenburg.  We congratulate
  29| the Town administration’s work to maintain the AA+ bond rating, and thank all of the town
  30| employees for their continued service.  We are grateful for the continued cooperation of the
  31| Town Manager and each department head to ensure that every question is answered.
  32| Terri Burchfield, Chair; Mark Erickson, Vice Chair; Jay Simeone, Clerk
  33| Hannah Anderson, John Henshaw, George Martin, Marc Luescher

   2|  HISTORICAL COMMISSION
   3| The Historical Commission consists of five appointed volunteers who meet once a month
   4| from September to June. The work of the Historical Commission is preservation and appreci-
   5| ation of all things historic in the Town of Lunenburg. The Historical Commission has through
   6| the past year been represented on the Architectural Preservation District Commission
   7| (APDC) by two of our members, Richard McGrath and Cullen Dwyer. They have worked
   8| with the other members of the APDC to finish the Design Standards of the APDC Bylaw.
   9| We are nearing completion of the Bronze Marker Project, which was made possible through
  10| the efforts of Eagle Scout Joshua Herrick of Lunenburg Troop 1728. All the houses in Lunen-
  11| burg’s National Register Historic District were surveyed for plaques (last available in 1987)
  12| and those homes missing plaques were purchased by the Historic Commission from the Erie
  13| Landmark Company. We are awaiting delivery. Installation will happen this spring and all the
  14| houses in our National Registered Historic District will finally get the recognition they de-
  15| serve.
  16| We continue to hold hearings and enact the Demolition Delay Bylaw of the Town for the de-
  17| struction of Historic properties. This past year we saw the loss of an 1800’s barn on West
  18| Townsend Road.
  19| Historical Commission Members:
  20| Rebecca Lantry, Richard McGrath, Cullen Dwyer, Jennifer Sanderson and Bill Lakso.

   2| INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY
   3| The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through
   4| the use of technology.  The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to
   5| communicate and collaborate, manage daily tasks, organize, store and share information, and
   6| generally conduct Town business more effectively.  We also provide the necessary infrastructure,
   7| equipment, software and expertise to comply with current regulations and methodology when
   8| interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   9| The Information Technology staff for the Town has remained constant this year, but we have
  10| added a new full time IT position for the Schools.  Following is an overview of our current IT
  11| staff:
  12| Steve Malandrinos – Information Technology Director
  13|  Hired in December, 2012
  14|  Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA
  15| and Belchertown Public Schools
  16| Daniel Nadareski – Network Administrator
  17|  Retired police officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job
  18|  Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video
  19| security system administration, and IP phone system administration
  20| Jordan Pochini – Tech Support Specialist
  21|  Recently graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  22|  Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail
  23| environments
  24| Nicholas Scoggins – School Technology Coordinator
  25|  Received Associate and Bachelor of Science degrees from ITT Technical Institute
  26|  Served 5 years as Security System Administrator at Greater Lawrence Technical School
  27| Along with serving as helpdesk for all users’ daily technology issues and performing regular
  28| maintenance and scheduled replacement of the town’s and schools’ technology assets, the IT
  29| Department has been involved with several high impact projects throughout the past year.
  30| Over the past few months, we have worked with an information security firm to perform a cyber-
  31| security audit of our user base.  The audit has consisted of several mock phishing campaigns,
  32| designed to test our users’ awareness of and reaction to attempts to illicitly gain access to Town
  33| computer systems. The goal of the audit is to raise awareness of one of the most prevalent
  34| malware vectors used to infiltrate computer networks, and with each iteration and subsequent
  35| training, our failure rate has improved significantly.
  36| We have also worked closely with the Town Manager on a complete upgrade and redesign of the
  37| Town website.  With citizen engagement top of mind, we are adding new ways to keep abreast of
  38| what is happening, as well as have your voice heard and get involved.  Our new homepage will
  39| prominently feature our official Facebook feed, and options to sign up for alerts when things
  40| change or new information is added.  Our goal is to keep the information flowing – in both
  41| directions – so that everyone has a chance to effectively participate in the process.

   2| LUNENBURG PUBLIC LIBRARY
   3| The Library had a very busy year (67,762 people came through our doors) but it has also been very fun!
   4| When I look back on the last year it seems centered on three actions:  COLLABORATE,
   5| COMMUNICATE and CREATE.  We have stepped things up in each of these three areas.  Collaborating
   6| with individuals and local organizations has been a primary focus of the Library this year – even if to
   7| make it happen we had to tweak a few of our more traditional rules!.   The Library partnered with Project
   8| Bread and its Meals4Kids program to bring nutritious lunches to children throughout the summer months.
   9| We have also begun to use our communication vehicles like our Facebook page and our website as well
  10| as leveraging the skills of some of the Library’s staff to help other organizations get the word out on a
  11| special event or activity and/or improve the look of their communications.   The Library provided space
  12| and assistance for the activities of local organizations like GiftMart, the Women’s Club Christmas Basket
  13| effort, Coloring for Cancer campaign, the Scouts and many more.  In recognition of the fact that more and
  14| more people go to school online the Library has been proctoring exams for some students (high school
  15| and college level).  The Library has created new traditions with our introduction of an Artisan’s Fair and
  16| Family Craft Night (cocoa & crafts after the Town Tree Lighting). The Library introduced a monthly e-
  17| newsletter so we can push the latest news and events out to our users.
  18| During the past year with the help of the Friends of the Library organization and several volunteers we
  19| provided two Book & Bake Sales, began reupholstering some “well-loved” furniture, and moved the
  20| entire (14,000+ books) adult collection to improve accessibility and make room for growth. The Library
  21| also introduced two new program series on Fundraising & Grants and Our Democracy. Funding provided
  22| by the Friends organization and the Cultural Council helped to provide 650 programs. The Library also
  23| hosted over 300 meetings and events for other local organizations.
  24| The Children’s Room continues to be a popular destination for children and families throughout the
  25| region.  Collection evaluation and development continues as we strive to provide the most up-to-date,
  26| substantive materials for our young patrons.  The beginning chapter book area that we created for
  27| emergent readers has outgrown its initial location and now has a new home with lots more room for
  28| display and expansion.  The non-fiction area for preschool aged children is heavily used and we are
  29| continually adding new items.  Nonfiction books give children a chance to learn new concepts and
  30| vocabulary, as well as broaden their view of the world.  Educators and publishers are recognizing that this
  31| interest begins at a very early age for many children; therefore, the book options are wonderful!
  32| We are constantly evaluating our toy/manipulative collection to keep it current, stimulating and in good
  33| condition.  Toys are more than just fun and games for kids.  Playing with toys engages a child’s senses,
  34| sparks their imaginations and encourages them to interact with others.  We are fortunate to have had many
  35| quality items donated for our use.
  36| We also have enrichment activities designed for school aged children.  Lego Club which is offered with a
  37| monthly Lego Challenge is well attended by both girls and boys and encourages child/parent interaction.
  38| Our Tuesdays with Stories Book Club also occurs monthly and the children are enjoying the lively book
  39| discussions and related snacks and crafts which are all part of this program.  Monthly S.T.E.M.
  40| programming is also provided for elementary aged children. Our goal with these fun science, technology,
  41| engineering and math activities is to encourage and excite these young learners.   A strong knowledge
  42| base in these core subjects - science, technology, engineering, and math (STEM) is one of the keys to a
  43| successful future.

   2| Usage of the Story Craft Room by Lunenburg Boy and Girl Scout Troops has increased.  These
   3| organizations have discovered that we provide a friendly, warm, clean meeting space with lots of parking.
   4| The added benefit to these children and their families is access to our wonderful library materials.
   5| The Teen Room saw increased attendance, new partnerships and more in 2017.  The amount of hours
   6| read for the 2017 Summer Reading Program doubled to a whopping 447 hours read in response to a
   7| change in the prize structure.  Program participation ran strong with 175 tweens and teens attending 14
   8| programs.
   9| A partnership with the Boy’s and Girl’s Club resulted in a new program, Homework Power Hour, where

  11| with a quiet space to study as well as access to computers.  In addition, National Honor Society students
  12| have the opportunity to earn service hours by tutoring.  This partnership also allowed for offering a Mad
  13| Science Escape Room as part of the Shrieks and Krieks Haunted House, bringing a drop in library
  14| program to those who are unable to attend the afterschool surprise sessions.

  16| nonfiction collection was interfiled with the adult collection.  This resulted in more shelf space for the

  18| On a final note, teen led programming was introduced in December with the plastic canvas holiday craft
  19| program.  While the library provided space and supplies, this workshop was created and led by a teen who
  20| wanted to share the needlepointing skill she had learned over the summer.  The hope is to introduce more
  21| of this type of programming in the future as it encourages public speaking, leadership skills, and fosters a
  22| sense of ownership in library.

   2| Studio and Equipment Upgrades
   3| PAC has had studio space in the TC Passios school since 2013.  We encourage the public to
   4| take advantage of the facilities and equipment offered.
   5| The strategy for the PAC studio in the upcoming Fiscal years includes space planning for a
   6| new studio and to continue improvements in the current studio. Part of the plan is to up-
   7| grade the current audio and lighting systems to increase the quality of the content produced.  We would
   8| like to increase visibility in the community by mainly encouraging more studio usage, as well as loaning out
   9| equipment to the public.
  10| In FY17 PAC acquired four new JVC cameras and a wireless microphone system for Town
  11| Hall.  The wireless mics allow boards and committees the ability to conduct workshop
  12| meetings in the Bilotta Room at the center conference table.  Providing this audio flexibility
  13| has enhanced board members participation with the public.  The new cameras are current-
  14| ly being used by the PAC staff.
  15| Remote live broadcasting was improved with the addition of a DataVideo converter, which
  16| transfers a live signal through the internet to the CASTUS server and out to the public on
  17| Ch 8 or Ch 9.  Live feeds are now possible from the PAC studio, the LMHS Auditorium, and
  18| the Public Safety Building.
  19| Equipment purchases accounted for 25% of the FY17 expenses.
  20| Staff, Producers and Volunteers
  21| In FY17, there was one full-time Program Coordinator town employee and four part-time
  22| staff, classified as Vendors (Budget Line Item: “Purchase of Services”).
  23| The Program Coordinator managed the day-to-day operation of the organization, including
  24| all production and programming schedules, equipment and services procurement, studio
  25| and inventory administration, advertising, and social media. Vendors provided video re-
  26| cording of committee meetings and additional video assignments, as needed.

===PAGE 68=== (plain, 30 lines)
   1| 68
   2| Of the 75% of locally produced shows on Ch 9, most were produced by public access staff.  Our resident
   3| top producer, Pete Lincoln of “Talkin’ Baseball”, continued to create new programs for airing on Ch 9
   4| through FY17.
   5| There was one community volunteer contributing late in FY17, recording live board/committee meetings,
   6| as well as a student volunteer recording a number of Field Hockey games.  There is an ongoing campaign
   7| through print and social media to obtain more volunteers to help with production.  In addition to
   8| volunteers, there also media outreach to solicit new producers to help create content.
   9|  Manpower (Salaries, Purchase of Services ) accounted for 55% of the FY17 expenses.
  10| Financials
  11| PAC received all of its income from Comcast in quarterly payments (August, November, February and
  12| May). The overall payments received for FY17 were $107,849.80.  These payments were deposited to

   2| SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   3| Est. 1998
   4| CALENDAR YEAR JAN. 1, 2017–NOV. 1, 2017
   5| The Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a five member committee appointed by the Board of Selectmen. The
   6| Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an opportunity to
   7| receive up to $1,000 off their property tax bill in the calendar year rather than the fiscal year.  Participants
   8| worked in various town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general office duties, custodial, library
   9| shelving, and kitchen assistance. For each hour worked a person received $10 and worked up to 100 hours be-
  10| tween January 1, 2017 and November 1, 2017 to receive the abatement.  Any hours worked beyond that were
  11| considered volunteer hours.
  12| There were nine Lunenburg residents who participated between January 1, 2017 to November 1, 2017.  These
  13| individuals, as a whole, accumulated 862 hours towards their abatements.
  14| Eligibility Requirements:
  15| Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  16| Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  17| Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse’s name who is also 60 years of age or older
  18| Meet the income guidelines of :
  19| One person household - $40,000 / Two person household - $60,000
  20| All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re- apply and be accepted
  21| annually for participation in the program.
  22| Earnings are exempt from state taxes only.  Earnings are subject to federal and FICA tax withholdings and are
  23| also subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Retirement or
  24| Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
  25| Departments who participated between January 1, 2017 – November 1, 2017:
  26| Board of Selectmen
  27| Board of Health
  28| Building Department
  29| Council on Aging
  30| DPW/Sewer Department
  31| Public Library
  32| School Department
  33| Treasurer

===PAGE 71=== (plain, 50 lines)
   1| 71
   2| SEWER COMMISSION
   3| The five member Sewer Commission was established at the May 6, 2006 Special Town Meeting.  At the first
   4| Commission meeting after the May 2016 election, on June 21, 2016, when the Commission elects officers for
   5| the new fiscal year, Carl Luck was elected to remain as Chairman, John Reynolds as Vice-Chair and Sue Bunish
   6| as Clerk. Both Carl Luck and John Reynolds were elected to 3 year terms at the May 2016 election.  There
   7| remained two open positions on the Commission after a failure to elect at the May election and Joseph Anderson
   8| stepped forward to fill one vacancy after appointment by the Selectmen and Commission on July 5, 2016. On
   9| July 19, 2016, Joan Pease was appointed by the Selectmen and Commission to fill the other vacancy. When Sue
  10| Bunish submitted her letter of resignation in July 2016, Richard St. Cyr was appointed to fill that vacancy on
  11| October 11, 2016. He remained until February 26, 2017, when he resigned to move out of town.  With Sue’s
  12| departure, Joe Anderson was elected to serve as Clerk for the Commission on July 26, 2016. At the May 2017
  13| election in, Troy Daniels was elected to the one-year term opened by the departure of Richard St. Cyr by write-
  14| in vote.
  15| The Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning for the future and
  16| developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies.  The Department of Public Works manages and
  17| maintains the operational side of the system.  Under the Town Charter, responsibility for the day-to day
  18| operation and care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager.
  19| Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 83% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to
  20| Leominster and the other 17% is discharged to Fitchburg.   There are currently 862 properties connected to the
  21| Lunenburg system and another 381 that have access to Town sewer but are not yet connected. During FY17, 44
  22| new accounts connected to town sewer.  The Commission billed out $763,329 in quarterly sewer bills and had
  23| $63,087 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners’ property tax bills for non-payment.   Eighty-six
  24| properties gained access to sewer with the completion of the SRF project in the Pratt St., Pleasantview Ave. and
  25| Lakeview Ave. areas of town and were assessed a betterment of $13,997.47 to pay down the debt owed by the
  26| Town for the SRF project.  One additional property was assessed a privilege fee in FY17 for increased usage as
  27| a multi-family residence. Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer
  28| project.
  29| The Commission continues contracting with Small Water Systems Services of Littleton (SWSS) for the
  30| Operations and Management of the ten pumping stations and sewer infrastructure. The Commission has been
  31| very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with SWSS, renewing the contract for a second
  32| three-year renewable contract when SWSS was awarded the bid in July 2016.  The new contract, effective
  33| 7/1/16, was expanded to include the generators in each pump station and quarterly meter readings for manual
  34| and irrigation accounts.
  35| In July 2016, with the new Grease Trap regulation in place, the Commission commenced the annual permit
  36| program with the new fee structure and requirements for annual permits, compliance and reporting. Lunenburg
  37| has eighteen food service establishments with grease traps and interceptors.  This regulation continues to be
  38| strongly enforced as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance
  39| costs of the sewer system.  While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal,
  40| individual home owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases
  41| everyone’s costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  42| At the May 2017 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward four articles, three of which related to the budget
  43| and stabilization funds. For the standard FY18 budget article, $560,169.06 was appropriated from Sewer
  44| Enterprise revenues, $10,285 was transferred from the Sewer Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund and $14,520
  45| from the Sewer Inflow/Infiltration Stabilization Fund and appropriated from Sewer Enterprise retained earnings
  46| the sum of $160,276.94 for a total appropriation of $745,251 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY18.
  47| $911,971.24 was included in appropriations from the General Fund to cover both general expenses and debt
  48| service on the betterments.  The goal of the Commission is to maintain a balanced budget and build up the
  49| Retained Earnings so that it may be used for major capital projects to maintain and improve the operating
  50| efficiency of the system. The second article transferred monies to the Reserve Capacity Stabilization Fund and

===PAGE 72=== (plain, 23 lines)
   1| 72
   2| the third article was to create a Stabilization fund for Capital Reserve. 3% of proposed revenues were transferred
   3| to Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund.  The fourth article was to accept c. 40, § 58 of the General Laws to
   4| impose a “municipal charges lien” on real property for unpaid sewer connection fees, Sewer Reserve Capacity
   5| fees, Sewer Re-connection fees, and Sewer Grease Trap permit fees owed the Town in the manner as provided
   6| for by MGL c. 40, § 58.
   7| Engineering consulting firms, Wright Pierce and Weston & Sampson have continued to assist the Commission
   8| in working to identify and reduce Inflow and Infiltration (I/I) and the effort continues to be the focus of the
   9| Commission going into FY18.
  10| The Commission would like to thank Sue Bunish and Richard St. Cyr for their service and welcomes Joe
  11| Anderson, Joan Pease and the return of Troy Daniels in FY17. Bob Ebersole continues to serve as the Board of
  12| Selectmen liaison and the Commission is very grateful for his knowledge and counsel.  The Commission would
  13| also like to thank DPW Director Jack Rodriquenz for the seamless and smooth operation of the Sewer Division
  14| of the DPW and Jim Breault (DPW-Facilities) who whenever called upon, provides his expertise and service to
  15| the Sewer Division.  They would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for their
  16| partnership and good working relationship with the DPW and especially with Business Manager, Barb Lefebvre,
  17| in providing valuable assistance with meter readings, ownership changes, and status updates.
  18| Sewer Commission as of February 2018:
  19| Joe Anderson, Chair
  20| Carl Luck, Vice Chair
  21| Joan Pease, Clerk
  22| John Reynolds, Member
  23| Troy Daniels, Member

   2| BOARD OF HEALTH
   3| The Board of Health Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building on the corner of Mass. Ave
   4| and Leominster Road.  Our office is on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right.  We can
   5| be reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.
   6| The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health,
   7| continued its mission to maintain its focus on public health and safety issues during 2017,
   8| reviewing Title V Inspection Reports, building permits, septic and well permits, nuisance
   9| investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public health related issues.
  10| The Board of Health oversees the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to moderate
  11| income families replace failed septic systems or connect to public sewer.  Anyone interested in
  12| finding out more about this program can contact the office.
  13| Homeland Security planning is ongoing.  Meetings continue between our agents, other Town
  14| departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner
  15| towns in our region.
  16| This year we processed applications for septic permit lot tests, new construction permits, new
  17| wells, repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits.  The Board continues its vigilance
  18| with regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints
  19| and its general mandate to protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  20| The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the
  21| Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous
  22| clinics are provided throughout the year to our residents.  These clinics provide inoculations,
  23| blood pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and dental screening. Fluoride treatment and
  24| dental checks of our youngsters in the school system are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  25| Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project.  Mosquito Control
  26| monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as
  27| EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer
  28| times of the year. Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:
  29| cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage at:
  30| http://www.cmmcp.org , or calling the Board of Health office.
  31| With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents
  32| recovering from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  33| Please contact our office in the Ritter Memorial Building for more information.
  34| Please remember that our Board Members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the
  35| citizens of Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your
  36| service. Contact the Board office at 582-4146 ext. 430.
