   2| TOWN OF LUNENBURG, MASSACHUSETTS
   3| FIRE DEPARTMENT
   4| The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lun enburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the
   5| highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
   6| In 2017 your Fire Department responded to 2107 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   7| Fires & Hazardous Conditions                     514                Emergency Medical      1022
   8| Inspections                  587                 Other                             20
   9| Fire Department staffing as of July 1, 2017  is 5 Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters an d Emergency Medical
  10| Technicians, plus 1 full time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator.  The Station is manned 7 days per week  from 6am-6pm
  11| with 2-3 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians , plus the Chief of Department and EMS  Coordinator weekdays.  The Station
  12| is also staffed two nights per week with 2 Firefighter/EMT’s.  This coverage is a combination of  Full Time personnel  in
  13| conjunction with 84  hours per week of per diem coverage.  The per diem coverage is provided by our C all Firefighter/EMT’s
  14| manning the Station at their hourly Call rate.
  15| Sunday through Thursday nights however, the Station is unmanned and all of our personnel respond from home on an
  16| “on call” basis.   This  adds several minutes or more to our response tim e as personnel must respond to the station, pick up the
  17| appropriate equipment and then respond the call.
  18| The call volume in 2017 was an overall increase of 3% over 2016.  We also experienced a 6% increase  in Emergency
  19| Medical related calls, which is cons istent with the growth we have seen over the last 10 years .   As our community continues to
  20| grow, the demand for emergency services grows along with it .  I must continue to state the need for  24 hour staffing in the
  21| Station.  Funding to maintain a minimum of 2 Firefighter/EMT’s or Paramedics in the Station 24 hours a day, 7 days a week is
  22| critical to maintain the high level of se rvice our Townspeople expect.   This would allow either the first ambulance or first piece of
  23| fire apparatus to respond immediatel y.  Not only would this reduce our response times to critical emergencies, it would also
  24| reduce the “wear and tear” on our Call members.   This coverage would in no way re duce the need for our  On Call personnel; it
  25| would only supplement their response and reduce response times.
  26| Staffing was slightly increased on July 1, 2017 with the addition of 1 Full Time member.  Lt. Christos Lekaditis joined
  27| the ranks of our full time staff and functions as our Assistant Fire Prevention and Training Officer.  Christos was a Call Firefighter
  28| with the Department for 10 years and also served as a Full Time Firefighter with the Devens Fire Department.  The addition of
  29| this full time position allowed us to slightly increase our coverage during the day time, and to begin cove ring two nights per week
  30| as well.  It should be noted that an additional 5 positions are needed to provide 2 personnel in the Station 24/7, plus an ad ditional
  31| member weekdays when most of our Call Personnel are out of town.
  32| As we plan towards additional staffing in the future, consideration for transitioning the Ambulance Service to the
  33| Paramedic Level of care must also be considered.  We currently operate two Ambulance s at the Advanced EMT Level, but must
  34| rely on outside agencies for assistance with full  Advanced Life Support Care.  Hiring Paramedic Level trained Firefighters and
  35| equipping the ambulances to that level will bring a higher level of care to our residents in a faster, more efficient way.
  36| Recruitment and Retention of our Call Firefighters continues to be one of the greatest challenges for the Department, and is my
  37| main reason to call for more permanent staffing in the Station.  Make no mistake, our Cal l personnel do an outstanding job, and
  38| their commitment never ceases to amaze me.  However, like many Call Departments we suffer from a continual turnover
  39|                    655 Massachusetts Ave
  40| Lunenburg, MA 01462
  41|        CHIEF
  42|  Patrick A. Sullivan
  43|  TEL: (978) 582-4155
  44|  FAX: (978) 582-4033

===PAGE 80=== (plain, 53 lines)
   1| 80
   2| in personnel.  Public Safety is a field where training and experience are critical.  A new Call Firefighter/EMT must
   3| attend over 340 hours of initial training, plus additional time in orie ntation and field training.  Members qualifying to drive Fire
   4| Apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and practical training.  On average, it takes a new member one
   5| and a half to two years to properly complete these requirements.  Unfor tunately, for a variety of  reasons we often do not retain
   6| members for more than 5-6 years.
   7| As an example, during 2016 -2017 6 of our most active Call Firefighters obtained Full time Firefighting Positions with
   8| other Departments, and another member joined the Armed Services.  While most have remained members of the Department,
   9| their availability to be “On Call” and cover per diem shifts has been significantly reduced.
  10| In 2017, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 587 Inspections .   This is in addition  to multiple fire drills and public
  11| education initiatives.  By law we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detector installations when a home is sold,
  12| as well as all new oil burner and propane gas installations.  Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be inspected
  13| for fire safety issues as well.   In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire protection,
  14| as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process.   Personnel also continue to provide fire safety education to not
  15| only children in our schools but adults as well.     Other Fire Safety Initiatives undertaken during 2017 include:
  16| - Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953  to assist with Fire Safety Education in
  17| both the Schools and with Senior Citizens.  This Grant provides funding for both materials and personnel to bring
  18| fire safety education to our most vulnerable populations.  Highlights of activity through this grant include:
  19| o Educational programs in basic fire safety in Grades K -3, including use of the Regional Fire  Safety
  20| Trailer.
  21| o Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging.  These included cooking and heating
  22| safety, a CPR Demonstration and Ice safety.
  23| - Participation in the Senior Center’s “Sand for Seniors” Program.  This program provides buckets of walkway sand
  24| for Senior Citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in the winter months.  In partnership with this
  25| program, Captain James Ricci delivers the san d to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for
  26| Smoke and Carbon Monoxide Detectors.  If the resident was lack ing detectors, Capt. Ricci is able to  install free
  27| detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  28| - It should be noted that eac h year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or nonexistent smoke
  29| detectors.
  30| Firefighters Joseph Arsenault and Stephen Morse graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting Academy’s
  31| Call/Volunteer Training Program.  This intensive 4 month pro gram provides the basic skills firefighters need to operate in today’s
  32| challenging environments.  All newly hired Firefighters are required to complete this program within one year of hire.
  33|  10 Firefighters from Lunenburg have undergone specialized trai ning and respond as part of the District Technical
  34| Rescue and Dive Teams.  These regional teams provide specially trained personnel  and equipment beyond what most
  35| communities are able to provide at the local level.   In conjunction with these Teams, Lunenb urg operates both a Water Rescue
  36| Unit and a Collapse/Trench Rescue Trailer.  In September, Chief Sullivan, Capt. Ken Jones and Firefighter Ben Boudreau
  37| participated in Operation SCORE, a Disaster Drill designed to test the capabilities of the State’s Regio nal Technical Rescue
  38| Teams.  Our members, along with 50+ other Firefighters from Worcester County traveled to Cape Cod and operated through the
  39| night at a simulated community destroyed  by a severe weather event.  Crews stabilized damaged structures  and conducted
  40| rescues of simulated victims.  This was the first full scale deployment and test of the Worcester County Teams.  Our Region’s
  41| Team was one of the few that was able to fully complete all tasks assigned to them, and completed them ahead of schedule.
  42| Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern .  Two of our front line fire apparatus (Engine 4 and Ladder 1) are 22
  43| and 29 years old  years old respectively.  Both units are showing  their age through increasing maintenance costs and “out of
  44| service” time.  These units have been submitted for replacement  on the FY’19  Capital Plan ( both having been turned down in
  45| FY’18) and it is hoped that due consideration will be given for their timely replacement.  Rescue 2, a 2008 Ambulance has also
  46| been submitted f or replacement as it is near the end of its projected life span.  The high reliability required of front line
  47| emergency apparatus becomes increasingly difficult to maintain as these units reach the end of their life span.
  48| In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their  outstanding commitment to
  49| the Town.  Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress me every day.  I would also like to thank the
  50| other department heads, board s and commiss ions for their continuing help.  Most people do not realize the level of
  51| interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in the daily course of town business.  My thanks also go out to the citizens of
  52| Lunenburg for their ongoing support of public safety .  It is that support that allows us to continue prov iding the services you
  53| expect from us.

  20| Retirements
  21| Linda C. Carrier
  22| Public Safety Coordinator
  23| Congratulations to Linda Carrier on her retirement!  On May 16, 1983, after being employed in the
  24| Town offices for a couple of years, Linda was appointed to the police department as the Chief’s
  25| Administrative Assistant, a job that would later be reclassified as the Public Safety Coordinator. On June
  26| 18, 1984 Linda would be appointed as a Reserve Intermittent Police Officer and then field train to
  27| become a certified Sexual Assault Investigator. I speak on behalf of the entire police department when I
  28| say it’s been an honor to work with Linda. Linda has served this community well with unwavering
  29| enthusiasm and passion for over 34 years. She will be sorely missed!  We here at the Police Department
  30| wish Linda happiness and health in her retirement.

   2| Citizen’s Police Academy
   3| I would like to take this opportunity to thank the Citizens of Lunenburg for making the second annual
   4| Citizen’s Police Academy a success. I am again grateful for the enthusiastic support of the Citizen
   5| Students. The program curriculum included: Criminal Law, Court Process, Firearms Familiarization,
   6| Use of Force and Defensive Tactics, Taser, Traffic Safety & Accident Investigation, K-9 Operations,
   7| Crime Scene Photography, Drug Investigations, and Latent Finger Print Recovery.
   8| I have nothing but praise the officers who volunteered their time to present these subjects; Dep. Chief
   9| Retired, Paul Bozicas, Clerk Magistrate Patrick Malone, Sgt. Sean Connery, Sgt. Tom Gammel, Officer
  10| Brad McNamara, Officer Josh Tocci and Det. Sgt. Jeff Thibodeau. A special thanks again this year to
  11| Lieutenant Mike Luth for organizing and facilitating the program. The next Citizen’s academy will be
  12| scheduled in the fall of 2018. I look forward to seeing you then.
  13| About the Police Department
  14| The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day 7 days a week.  Two patrol officers are assigned to
  15| each shift with support from the Traffic Enforcement/K-9 Officer who works two day shifts and two
  16| evening shifts. Also supporting the patrol unit is the Criminal Investigation Unit headed by a Detective
  17| Sergeant. The Community Outreach officer, who is also assigned as the School Resource Officer comes
  18| under the auspices of the C.I.U. Unit The Lieutenant and Public Safety Coordinator are on duty Monday
  19| thru Friday between the hours of 7Am and 3Pm. Office hours for the Chief of Police are 8Am to 4Pm
  20| Monday thru Friday. The Animal Control Officer, who is under auspices of the Police Department, is on
  21| call.
  22| The front desk is open Monday thru Friday from 7AM to 7PM.  Emergency, Non-emergency and 911
  23| calls are routed through Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District, (NVRDD). For all emergencies dial
  24| 911. For general calls please call (978) 582-4531.
  25| Applications for Firearms Licensing can be found on the Police Department Website at
  26| lunenburgma.gov. Contact (978) 582-4531 for an appointment.

   2| The public is reminded to report any animal related complaints to the Regional Call Center at (978)

   8| The State of the Police Department
   9| The Police responded to 17048 calls for service this year, a 2.2% increase over last year; responded to
  10| 301 motor vehicle accidents, investigated 1282 incidents, 44 Field Interview Reports, wrote 2236 motor
  11| vehicle citations, arrested 193 people, summonsed 175, and placed 13 people in protective custody. The
  12| K-9 was deployed 27 times.  In the first half of fiscal 2017 staff assignments had to be increased 75
  13| times to effectively meet service demands.
  14| The day shift desk officer answered 2083 calls and took 1402 lobby inquiries and complaints between
  15| July and December of 2017. The day shift desk officer wrote 93 incident reports and 3 field interview
  16| reports in 2017.  This year the day shift desk watch officer was responsible for conducting suicide watch
  17| and assisting with the court processing of 66 detainees; either taken into custody overnight or during the
  18| day shift. The day shift desk officer processed 134 Firearms Licenses, 11 Non FID related finger prints,
  19| and registered 13 Sex Offenders. The 3pm to 7pm desk watch officers assisted in the processing of, 50
  20| detainees while providing suicide watch and numerous other services as noted in the job description
  21| below.
  22| I would like to take this opportunity to thank the Town for supporting the staff increase that I requested
  23| in the FY 18 budget. The addition of one officer enabled the Police Department to create a School
  24| Resource Officer Position. An outpouring of input from the community, and the shift in societal values
  25| and dynamic social change has combined to influence the decision to create such an important job.
  26| Growth revenue provided the funding for the additional officer and although the School Resource
  27| Officer position is important to ease the concerns of crime and violence in schools, because the Officer
  28| is committed to the School District for at least 10 months, it does not help the Police Department keep
  29| pace with the demands for service. This year, although arrests are down, calls for service continue to rise
  30| causing the work load to grow.
  31| In the past twenty years the police department has not seen any significant increase in staffing despite a
  32| significant increase in community development and growth. For several years I have asked for three
  33| officers per year until the police department reaches an appropriate number of full time officers to meet
  34| the work load demand. I have proven through work load demand studies that this police department
  35| should employ between twenty-three (23) and twenty-six (26) officers. The studies agree with
  36| Department of Justice assessments which indicate that based upon our population the Lunenburg Police
  37| Department should employ 2.6 officers per thousand populations.
  38| In past presentations I have publicized town reports dating back to 1996 that makes it clear the number
  39| one priority has been to increase the size of the police department. Recently I discovered a letter from
  40| the previous police chief addressed to the then town manager dated April 23, 2006. The letter indicated
  41| that the police department employed 13 full time officers including the Chief of Police, today it employs
  42| 15. The letter pointed out that a survey conducted by the Bureau of Justice Assistance revealed that 21
  43| full time police officers for every 10,000 residents served.  The Police Chief reports in the letter that a
  44| Massachusetts Police Employment Survey revealed an average of 2.1 police officers per 1000 residents
  45| served with some 3.0 and the lowest 1.8 officers per thousand residents served. Attached to this letter

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                       was a local police officer staffing survey which revealed an average population of 12,200 had a staffing
   2|                       level of 21 officers. Most of the towns in the 10000 population range had 18, 19, 21 full time officers
   3|                       employed.
   4|                       I will be repeating myself once again with a request for staffing increases in the FY19 budget. I remind
   5|                       those who have control over the budget that the service the public expects will continue to decline due to
   6|                       inadequate employment policies over a long period of time. Once again I will take a quote from last
   7|                       year's budget memo, "the time to properly investigate crimes, solve problems by engaging the
   8|                       community, or providing a quick emergency response is continually at odds with the demands of
   9|                        growth. The community will eventually see the police department as a useless ineffective service that is
  10|                       a drain on their income and their support for the Police will decline." Again, knowing the challenges
  11|                       just to approach level funding, e.g. contractual pay increases, net increases in supplies, etc., I encourage
  12|                       you to fund an incremental increase in staffing over the next several years utilizing growth revenue.

  12|                       Incidents and arrests declined this year but are still on the rise over the past five and ten years. Calls for
  13|                       service increased which may account for the decrease in arrests. Crime is down this year however;
  14|                       crimes against the person are down 7%, property crimes are down 16%, drug and gun crimes are down
  15|                       41%, and group B crimes, e.g. all other offenses, check fraud, drunk driving, disorderly conduct,
  16|                       trespassing, liquor law violations are down by 26%.
  17|                       Calls for service will continue to rise as the community grows. Crime is unpredictable--a moving target-
  18|                       - which makes budgeting very challenging. A single catastrophe and can wipe out a police overtime
  19|                       budget. Being prepared, however, is the key to a successful law enforcement agency and I can't
  20|                       emphasize enough how important staffing is with respect to being adequately prepared. The community
  21|                       has to be willing to support the cost of preparedness.
  22|                       I can't emphasize enough how important it is to keep with current growth trends with respect to staffing
  23|                       the police department. Growth, historically plays a major role in calls for service, arrests, incident and
  24|                       motor vehicle accidents. The demands for service will continue to rise over time. I respectfully ask for
  25|                       your support with funding request so that the Police Department can keep up with the demands of
  26|                       growth and offer the service the public expects and is entitled to.
  27|                       I thank you again for allowing me to manage your Law Enforcement agency another year. I appreciate
  28|                       all citizen input and value the support of the public. Please be diligent about reporting crimes. Let's
  29|                       embrace the concept of, "see something, say something."

   1| 90
   2| Lunenburg’s Charter specifically mandates a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year Plan and Capital
   3| Improvement Plan to be presented to the Town Manager. The criterion for any capital request includes mandates,
   4| safety, need, crisis, cost effectiveness and ability to fund. The committee has to review each request under these
   5| six elements. Not only do they have to determine that the request fits within the criteria, but they have to prioritize
   6| the level that meets the criteria. Therefore, not all requests are included in the fiscal year plans, mostly due to
   7| funding.
   8| At the Annual Town Meeting in May 2017, the following items totaling $633,317, were
   9| presented in the FY18 plan.

   2|                        Zoning Board of Appeals
   3| The Zoning Board of Appeals heard pe titions during calendar year 2017.   There were several applicants seek ing special
   4| permits and special permits for dimensional variations.  The Zoning Board meetings are held on the second and fo urth
   5| Wednesdays of the month on an “as needed” basis.
   6| The Zoning Board consists of the following members:  Alfred Gravelle, Chairman, James Besarkarski, Vice Chairman,
   7| Members: Robert Cadle, Paul Doherty and Hans Wentrup and Associate members, David Blatt, and Sheila Lumi.   Lisa
   8| Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
   9| The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  10| 1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town’s Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  11| 2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town’s Protective By-laws.
  12| 3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  13| amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action
  14| from the Administrative Officer or  Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or
  15| other Administrative Official.
  16| 4. To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter
  17| 40B.  The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews re quests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under
  18| Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.  These requests can, by law, circumvent local
  19| zoning ordinances.
  20| The o ffice of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue ,
  21| Lunenburg.  Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00 PM Tuesday and
  22| Thursday, closed Friday.  If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146
  23| X 421 during regular business hours.  Zoning Board applications are available in the office and o nline at
  24| www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

   2| Lunenburg Public Schools
   3| 2017-2018
   4| Through its stated Mission and Core Values, The Lunenburg Public Schools provides its
   5| students with an understanding of, and a responsible commitment to the principles of
   6| freedom, respect, personal worth and a concern for each other.
   7| The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills,
   8| confidence and passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to
   9| successful careers, active citizenship and rewarding lives.
  10| The Core Values of the Lunenburg Public Schools
  11| • Quality education cultivates creative problem solving, independent thinking
  12|    and multiple points of view.
  13| • Effective education addresses the whole child, involve authentic engagement,
  14|    and provide multiple   pathways to success.
  15| • Students must be provided with an academically and personally safe
  16|    environment that promotes effective learning.
  17| • Students should be provided with opportunities to develop initiative and
  18|    responsibility which will help them become active and supportive community
  19|    members.
  20| • Focused, sustained professional development and reflective practice are
  21|    fundamental to excellence in teaching.
  22| • Strong collaboration and open communication among school staff, students,
  23|   families and the community develop mutual respect and trust, and enhance
  24|   students’ ability to succeed.
  25| • Resourcing decisions should be responsible, address critical needs and put
  26|   students first.

  20| Superintendent’s Message
  21| I am pleased to report another engaging and productive year of teaching and learning in
  22| the Lunenburg P ublic Schools. Typically I would review our state assessment results in
  23| this report, but our District did not receive an accountability rating this year since
  24| students in grades 3 -8 participated in 2017 Next Generation MCAS tests.  Students
  25| received indivi dual results and the high school received the highest accountability
  26| ranking.  The next -generation MCAS is a reformatted test, and as such the scores are not
  27| comparable to the prior tests.  The next -generation MCAS emphasizes readiness for
  28| higher-level work at the next grade level and has different performance categories.
  29| To support the students our faculty engaged in focused professional development and
  30| curriculum renewal to ensure alignment with the new rigorous State standards and
  31| assessments consistent with the new strategic plan.   After a year -long development effort
  32| involving the entire community, in the fall of 2017 the School Committee adopted a new
  33| mission, vision and strategic plan, based upon the core values identified in the planning
  34| process.
  35| Strategic Plan 2018-2023
  36| Goal 1: Social-emotional learning, prevention programs and secure schools provide safe
  37| environments, promote kindness and celebrate cultural differences.
  38| Goal 2: Civic learning and engagement enable all students to become knowledgeable
  39| citizens and active community members.
  40| Goal 3: Curricula and assessments in major subject areas are aligned with recent
  41| Massachusetts State Frameworks and fully implemented.

===PAGE 98=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 98
   2| Goal 4: Implementation of digital literacy and computer science curr iculum is expanded;
   3| and, instructional technologies are more available.
   4| Goal 5:  The processes for annual professional development ensure opportunities for
   5| well-targeted individual, school-based, and district-wide professional growth.
   6| Goal 6: Strategies to improve instructional support for struggling learners are enhanced.
   7| Goal 7: Enrichment opportunities are expanded to include the arts and other areas of high
   8| student interest.
   9| Goal 8: Collaboration and communications within and among the schools and the
  10| community are improved.
  11| The first year update on progress toward the above goals will be posted on the District
  12| website in June 2018.
  13| We are in the process of closing out a very successful building project.  The Lunenburg
  14| Middle-High School finished on time.   The approved projected project budget was
  15| $72,590,848.   At the time of writing this report, the project is projected to come in
  16| approximately $4,477,355 under the projected budget.
  17| This fall we opened all the new grass fields.  Coupled with the re furbished track and field
  18| facility, this outdoor facility is second to none in the region.  Our coaches and athletes
  19| were excited for the opportunity to practice and play on -site after the loss of most of the
  20| fields during construction.  This fall we also welcomed to our school campus Lunenburg
  21| youth and adult sports teams.  Our Adult Education/Lifelong Learning program continues
  22| to grow and expand.  Five new activities were added to the program this year including
  23| educational presentations on the opioid cr isis offered in conjunction with the Lunenburg
  24| Resources for Opioid Awareness and Reco very Task Force (ROAR).  The partnership
  25| between the School Department, the Lifelong Learning Program, and the youth sports
  26| programs is an example of the positive outcome  of a community working together to
  27| meet the needs of all its citizens.
  28| Abigail L. Dwyer received the 2017 Superintendent’s Award.  Ms. Dwyer is the daughter
  29| of Todd and Jacqueline Dwyer.  During her four years at LHS Abigail enrolled in a
  30| rigorous cou rse of studies taking honors and advanced placement courses.   She was
  31| active in the arts as a vocalist and thespian. Ms. Dwyer was also active as a volunteer in
  32| her school and community while also working part -time.  In college she will study
  33| sociology an d mathematics and then plans to attend law school. I was honored to
  34| recognize this accomplished young woman.
  35| I am extremely pleased to announce our affiliation with the North Central Massachusetts
  36| Early Literacy Innovation Zone.   The Lunenburg Public Schools in conjunction with the
  37| Community Foundation of North Central Massachusetts, the United Way of North
  38| Central Massachusetts, Fitchburg State University,  Footsteps2Brilliance and nine other
  39| districts in the area have joined together to create a collective impact, region-wide
  40| literacy to address school readiness with the goal of all students reading on grade level by
  41| third grade.   Two applications are available to any family residing in Lunenburg at no
  42| cost.   Using mobile technology, families have access to a comprehensive curriculum of
  43| books, songs, and games in English and Spanish.  The curriculum can be downloaded and
  44| access off line.    We are excited to be part of this regional effort.   Information about how

  10| School Facilities
  11| The first year of operation of the new Lunenburg Middle -High School included training
  12| on the new systems in the school, discovering warranty items that needed to be addressed
  13| and collecting data on the school operating cost.  The school relies heavily on t echnology
  14| to operate efficiently and the maintenance staff continued to learn about the heating and
  15| ventilation controls, lighting system, security camera and door access system, telephone
  16| system and fire safety system throughout the year. Between the vari ous contractors and
  17| the school maintenance personnel mostly all issues were rapidly resolved.  One
  18| significant finding in late February 2017 was that snow sliding on the pitched roofs
  19| toward the center of the A and D wings had the potential to do great dam age to roof top
  20| equipment and to roof penetrations.  In July 2017, roof guards were added to the A and D
  21| building by Greenwood Industries at selected locations to protect those items that were
  22| felt to be at risk from sliding snow.  An unresolved issue with  rooftop ventilation units
  23| that caused the units to shut down at start up during temperatures below 20 degrees,
  24| continues to be worked on by the contractors and equipment manufacturer and is
  25| expected to be resolved by the end of March 2018.
  26| The roof top so lar project for Lunenburg Middle -High School was completed in June
  27| 2017 which will produce 335,000 kilowatt hours in the first year of operation at a fixed
  28| price for the next 20 years at .83 cents per kilowatt.  This represents an estimated 40% of
  29| the scho ol electricity needs. This roof top unit coupled with two power purchasing
  30| agreements through the town lead to a significant reduction in cost to operate the school
  31| and resulted in all electricity needs for the school district being provided by solar power.
  32| The focus of school maintenance for FY18 continues on preventative maintenance on
  33| heating and ventilation systems and energy conservation measures.  Funding for
  34| conversion of Turkey Hill Elementary School to LED lighting and building envelope
  35| tightening will be high priorities for Lunenburg Schools in the March 2018 Green
  36| Communities Competitive Grant round.
  37| The FY 18 capital program funded additional asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill
  38| Elementary School. In July and August 2017, Encore Contracting Ser vices for $168,079
  39| removed spray -on fire proofing above the ceiling in the first floor hallway and one
  40| stairwell of the classroom wing.  This project included removal of the splined ceiling and
  41| the floor tiles in the hallway, as well as the flooring in the  administration area and the
  42| connecting corridors. Turkey Hill Elementary School is expected to continue to have
  43| additional asbestos removal planned for a number of years.
  44| Respectfully submitted,
  45| John Londa, Director of Facilities and Grounds

===PAGE 101=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 101
   2| Technology Department
   3| Technology touches all our lives every day at the Lunenburg Public Schools. It is our aim
   4| in IT to serve our students, faculty, and staff proactively, so that the tools used to
   5| facilitate learning function seamlessly and responsively when needed. From checking
   6| email, to accessing class information, to connecting and collaborating with colleagues
   7| near and far, faculty, staff, and IT are working together to help redefine what is possible
   8| for our students, so they can redefine what’s poss ible for the world. Our annual report
   9| highlights the IT team, as well as some of the improvements we have made this past year
  10| to achieve this goal.
  11| The IT Team
  12| Our team continues to learn and grow each year as we improve our infrastructure and
  13| explore innovations in education technology.
  14| Steve Malandrinos – Information Technology Director
  15|  Five years working for the Town and Schools
  16|  Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of
  17| Belchertown, MA and Belchertown Public Schools
  18| Daniel Nadareski – Network Administrator
  19|  Retired Police Officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on
  20| the job
  21|  Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration,
  22| video security system administration, and IP phone system administration
  23| Nicholas Scoggins – School Technology Coordinator
  24|  Received Associate and Bachelor of Science degrees from ITT Technical Institute
  25|  Served 5 years as Security System Administrator at Greater Lawrence Technical
  26| School
  27| Jordan Pochini – Tech Support Specialist
  28|  Graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  29|  Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in
  30| retail environments
  31| Spurred on by the need for capacity for computer based testing, our student-to-device
  32| ratio continues to improve year after year.  We strive to put as many devices into
  33| students’ hands as possible, with the goal of making the improvements sustainable in
  34| following years always top of mind.  In the spring of 2016, our districtwide student-to-
  35| device ratio was approximately 4.2 to 1.  As of now, it stands around 1.4 to 1 and
  36| continues to drop each year due to district-funded improvements as well as generous
  37| donations from individuals and organizations like the PTO.
  38| Our ultimate goal remains to provide the tools necessary for faculty, staff, and students
  39| alike to facilitate the learning process in a secure, highly-available, and high-performing
  40| environment, and to always just ‘make IT work’.
  41| Respectfully Submitted,
  42| Steve Malandrinos, Information Technology Director

   2| Special Services Department
   3| FY17 (data taken from 2016-2017 school year)
   4| From the March 1, 2017 report, there are 275 students with disabilities receiving
   5| special education or related services in Lunenburg (last  year there were 299).  This
   6| represents 16.9% of our total student population of 1,628 students (it was 18.8% last
   7| year).  The state average from the 2017 school report card is 17.4%.  As of March 1,
   8| 2017, the total number of students receiving services ou tside the district was 14 (last year
   9| was 19).  Of the 14 students, 2 are placed in Collaborative Placements, 8 are in Day
  10| Placements and 4 are in Residential Placements.  Additionally, from the March 1, 2017
  11| report, 103 students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in
  12| Lunenburg (last year was 114).
  13| This year as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is NO
  14| LEVEL with the Primary School (Insufficient data), Turkey Hill Elementary School (No
  15| level), Lunenburg Middle School (No level), and Lunenburg High School (Level 1).  The
  16| state report card lists the district’s determination of need for Special Education technical
  17| assistance or intervention for Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as “Meets
  18| Requirements – At Risk (MRAR)”.  Students with Disabilities participated in MCAS this
  19| year with the following rates:  English Language Arts = 99% (it was 99% last year),
  20| Mathematics = 99% (It was 100% last year), and Science = 97% (It was 100% last year).
  21| We “Met Tar get” for all areas of participation.  This data can be found at the DESE
  22| website:
  23| http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/accountability/report/district.aspx?linkid=30&orgcode=0162
  24| 0000&orgtypecode=5&
  25| Other data collected throughout the year is also important to consider when
  26| looking at the whole Special Services Department.  DESE requires us to collect data for
  27| certain Indicators they are targeti ng each year.  For our Indicators, the following
  28| information is available below and also can be found at:
  29| http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx?orgcode=01620000&
  30| Indicator 1  – Graduation Rate. For the 2015 -2016 school year, the state target for
  31| Students with Disabilities was 84% (was 82.0% last year). Lunenburg’s rate was 73.9%
  32| (was 66.7% last year) and the overall state rate was 71.8% (was 69.9% last year) for
  33| Students with Disabilities.
  34| Indicator 2 – Dropout Rate.  For the 2015-2016 school year, the state target for Students
  35| with Disabilities was 2.7% (was 3.0% last year). Lunenburg rate for Students with IEPs
  36| was 1.8% (it was 0% last year and 2.2% the year befo re) and the overall state rate was
  37| 2.6% (was 2.9% last year).
  38| Indicator 3C – Proficiency Rate for Students with IEPs on MCAS.  For the 2016 MCAS,
  39| Lunenburg Students with Disabilities CPI ratings were as follows:

  12| Indicator 4  – Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs.  For
  13| Lunenburg, our rate was .7% (last year it was 0%).  The state rate was .7%.
  14| Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant discrepancies with the
  15| Suspension/Expulsion rates by race or ethnicity.  In 2016, Lunenburg was not found to
  16| have any significant discrepancies due to these factors.
  17| Indicator 5  -Educational Environments for Students Aged 6 -21 with IEPs. For 2015 -
  18| 2016, the state’s rates are as follows:  for Full Inclusion of students = 71.9%, Partial
  19| Inclusion = 16.2%, Substantially Separate Placements = 7.5%, and for Separate
  20| Schools/Residential F acilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 4.4%.  In Lunenburg, our
  21| percentages are as follows:  Full Inclusion = 67.7, Partial = 14.7%, Substantially  Separate
  22| = 9.0% and Separate schools, Residential, etc. = 8.6%.
  23| Indicator 6  – Educational Environments for Students  Aged 3-5 with IEPs.  The 2015-
  24| 2016 state rates showed the percent of students receiving a majority of their special
  25| education and related services in a Full Inclusion early childhood program was 53.1%, in
  26| a Partial Inclusion Program was 21.6%, a Substanti ally Separate Placement (special
  27| education class, separate school, or residential facility) was  15.4%, and students not
  28| attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech,
  29| OT, PT) was 9.9%. In Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 27.8%, our
  30| Partial Inclusion rate was 33.3%, Substantially Separate rate was 19.4%, and Related
  31| Services was 19.4%.
  32| Indicator 8 – Parent Involvement.  This indicator measures the % of parents with a child
  33| receiving special educa tion who report that school facilitated parent involvement as a
  34| means of improving services and results for students with IEPs.  For 2015 -2016 the state
  35| target was 85%.  Lunenburg’s rate was 78.3% and the state’s rate was 79.7% (from
  36| information collected in the Spring of 2013).
  37| Indicator 9 & 10  -Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special
  38| Education and in Special Education Categories.  In Lunenburg, the state determined that
  39| there was no disproportionality found in either case.
  40| Indicator 11 –Initial Evaluation Timelines.   T his indicator measures the % of children
  41| with parental consent to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established
  42| timeline. Data is reported for all initial evaluations initiated in October, November , and
  43| December of the reporting year.  Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance and the state
  44| average for this Indicator was 100%.

===PAGE 104=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 104
   2| Indicator 12  –Early Childhood Transition.  T his indicator measures the % of children
   3| referred by Part C, found eligible for s pecial education services, and who have an IEP
   4| developed and implemented by their third birthdays. The state target for this indicator in
   5| 100% and Lunenburg is noted as “Suppressed”.  Data is suppressed if the number of
   6| students referred by Part C and foun d eligible for special education services is fewer than
   7| 10.  In Lunenburg, only 2 students were referred during 2012 -2013 (we have not been
   8| reviewed since 2012-2013 for updated data in this area).
   9| Indicator 14 – Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs .  This indicator measures
  10| the % of students with IEPs  who exited high school during the 2014 -15 school year and
  11| self-reported post-school engagement in education or employment one year after leaving
  12| high school. Engagement is defined through three measurements:
  13|  Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education wi thin one year of leaving high
  14| school.
  15|  Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within
  16| one year of leaving high school.
  17|  Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary
  18| education or training program; or competitively employed, or in some other
  19| employment within one year of leaving high school.
  20| Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters
  21| engaged in a post -secondary activity one year after leaving high school. F or the 2015 -
  22| 2016 school year, state targets for the three measurements of Post -School Outcomes are
  23| Measurement A: 49%; Measurement B: 84%; and Measurement C:  91%.  Lunenburg
  24| rates are as follows (from reporting year 2014 -15):  Measurement A = 50%,
  25| Measurement B = 75%, and Measurement C = 75%.
  26| The Parent Advisory Council Supporting All Learners (PACSAL) continues to be
  27| an active support group for parents, teachers and administration.  All parents and
  28| community members can join the group, which currently consists of parents of students
  29| receiving Title I services and parents of students with disabilities.  Meetings are typically
  30| held the first Friday of each month, 11:00 A.M., in the Planning Room at the Middle
  31| School.  The executive board is made up of Chairperson: Kelly Harvey. Several events
  32| and presentations were organized by the PACSAL for both parents and teachers in
  33| Lunenburg this year. For more information, go to:
  34| https://sites.google.com/a/lunenburgschools.net/special-services/pacsal
  35| In conclusion, the Lunenburg Public Schools continues to provide a variety of
  36| special needs services to meet the varying needs of our students in the least restrictive
  37| environment.  We  have an experienced, hard working staff that works together with
  38| parents to provide these varied services.   Our parents are equal partners in our efforts to
  39| develop challenging and focused educational programs for children.  We remain
  40| committed to the Lu nenburg Public Schools mission, “The mission of the Lunenburg
  41| Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence, and passion for life -
  42| long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active
  43| citizenship, and rew arding lives.” This is a total community effort and we encourage
  44| ALL Lunenburg residents to join with us to create positive experiences and futures for
  45| our children.

  38| Lunenburg Primary School
  39|        The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students
  40| Preschool to 2 nd grade.  Our students range from three years old to eight years old. Our
  41| community is made up of students, parents, teachers and support staff; working together
  42| to create an educationally rich and safe learning environment that focuses on the whole
  43| child.

===PAGE 106=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 106
   2| The October 1, 2017 student count reported to the Department of Elementary and
   3| Secondary Education was three hundred and ninety -seven students.  Our Preschool
   4| program services forty -two three and four year old students. We have five Kindergarten
   5| classes servicing one hundred and nine students. Class sizes in our Kindergartens are at
   6| 21 to 22 students.  This past year we added a 1 st grade teacher in order to keep class sizes
   7| to 20 -21. We have one hundred and twenty 1 st grade students. First grade is a critical
   8| learning year for Reading and Writing and we feel that lowering class sizes will allow us
   9| to focus on individual student learning.  Second grade has one hundred and twenty - three
  10| students with class sizes ranging from 24 to 25 students.
  11|      Our professional development this year centered on Responsive Classroom and
  12| Emotional Intelligence for the second year. All of our classroom teachers are working on
  13| improving the use of Responsive Classroom Learning Curriculum which includes
  14| morning meetings, guided discovery, rules, logical consequences and academic choice.
  15|      To strengthen our commitment to focusing on the whole child and the Social
  16| Emotional Learning (SEL) needs of our children, teachers and staff have received
  17| professional developmen t on Responsive classroom and Emotional Intelligence.  Mrs.
  18| Nancy Chapdelaine has led our educators through in -service lessons for the second year
  19| in a row focusing this year on Rules in School. This year RULER was rolled in the
  20| classroom. Students and the ir families are learning about Emotional Intelligence, they
  21| have developed Charters to live and work by, instituted Mood Meters to assist students to
  22| learn more about emotions and the strategies to use to shift to a more productive place for
  23| learning. They have learned about a meta-moment and a blue print for conflict resolution.
  24|      We are dedicated to making the Lunenburg Primary School one that focuses on
  25| helping our community of students, staff and parents understand their emotions and how
  26| we bring our best selves to the learning we do each and every day.  Information on these
  27| two SEL’s can be found at our  website or by going to
  28| https://www.responsiveclassroom.org/ and http://ei.yale.edu/
  29|      The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered
  30| instructional system to insure that all students have the services they need to be
  31| successful. We have a growing English Learning Population of 2. 3% of our student
  32| population; Thirty-five percent of our students are considered to be high needs students
  33| according to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education, and 17.6% are
  34| economically disadvantaged students.  Common assessments are complet ed in the fall,
  35| winter, and spring for all children in grades K -2 in the areas of reading, writing, and
  36| math.  These common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher
  37| instruction and drive individual student instruction.  Struggling students  are identified
  38| quickly and additional resources and instruction are provided to address the areas of
  39| concern identified.  Tiered instructional services are provided in the area of Language
  40| Arts and Math.  The tiered instructional model requires that the t eachers not only meet
  41| the needs of the students who may be struggling with learning but those students who are
  42| doing well and need to achieve higher levels of learning.  Learning is more personalized
  43| and students are being challenged to be confident indepe ndent learners and problem
  44| solvers.

===PAGE 107=== (plain, 41 lines)
   1| 107
   2|      Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and
   3| supportive of each of our members. We have high expectations, insist on respectful
   4| behavior, and recognize the importance of build ing a community where members feel a
   5| sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute.  Our students have maintained a high
   6| level of community engagement and civic learning that is fostered through service
   7| learning projects at each grade level.  The Kin dergarten students learn about our local
   8| food bank and gift mart and sponsor school-wide can and gift drives through-out the year.
   9| Our 2nd grade students have learned about our local veterans and organized Valentines to
  10| go to area Veteran’s Hospitals.  St udents in the First grade have committed their energy
  11| to support our local animal shelters.  For the last several years we participate in Trick -or-
  12| Treat for UNICEF as a school, collecting a little over four hundred and fifty-two dollars.
  13|      The Primary S chool Parent/Teacher Advisory Council meets each month to discuss
  14| school related concerns and activities.  They review the annual budget for the school and
  15| provide advice for the administration on the school’s parent handbook and School
  16| Improvement Plan.

   2| The graph below provides a comparison of our school’s achievement on the Next
   3| Generation MCAS to other elementary schools in Massachusetts. We believe that moving
   4| to a computer based platform and more rigorous testing expe ctations holds challenges,
   5| but we are working to improve students’ preparedness.
   6| Professional development has focused on aligning the curriculum across all subject areas
   7| so that students will have common experiences at each grade level.   We  continue to
   8| refine the Teacher Support Team protocol, which examines struggling students’
   9| academic/personal histories and focuses on the best interventions. Our aim is not only to
  10| support a teacher’s current concerns, but also to streamline access to impo rtant
  11| background information and most successful strategies for next year’s teachers.  It is
  12| imperative to identify where students begin the year academically and catapult them
  13| forward as far as educationally sound strategies can bring them to the end of the year.  All
  14| students are assessed using the Developmental Reading Assessment (DRA) and Aimsweb
  15| Plus for both math and reading more than once throughout the year. This helps us
  16| monitor individual student progress.  We rely in part upon the expertise of ou r full time
  17| reading specialist, who works with Title 1 students and provides support and consultation
  18| for classroom teachers.

  20| success as academic learning.  To that end, we embraced a CARES motto, which focuses
  21| on Civility, Achievement, Respectfulness, Effort, and Sportsmanship. Every grade level

   2| team has created a student learning and professional practice goal related to teaching,

===PAGE 113=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 113
   2| Lunenburg Middle School
   3| Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong
   4| programs that support students’ social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that
   5| each child becomes a self -motivated, creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating
   6| environment that encourages diversity and open honest communication.   The dedicated
   7| and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philosophy of
   8| educating the whole child  academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a
   9| means by which all students become positive community members that possess the skills
  10| to be successful in high school. Lunenburg Middle School is in its second year as a co -
  11| located middle- high school serving grades six through eight in a beautiful new facility.
  12| Middle School students have experienced some new opportunities with the new middle
  13| school, such as foreign language and foreign language exploratory for all grade levels as
  14| well as a Cul tures class for eighth grade students. This year, all students in the middle
  15| school are participating in a program called Genius Hour. Genius Hour was developed to
  16| foster passion for learning back into the curriculum by having students facilitate their
  17| own learning based on their interests.  We will continue to seek out opportunities for
  18| students that foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high
  19| school and beyond or what their passion may lead them to.
  20| % of Students scoring in the meeting or exceeding expectations
  21| range Spring 2017 MCAS

  24| 6 MATH 66% 68 50%

  30| In reviewing the results of the 2017 Spring M.C.A.S. tests, our percentage of students
  31| scoring in the meeting or exceeding  expectations range remains strong and above the
  32| state average in all tested areas in the middle school. The Department of Elementary and
  33| Secondary Education held schools harmless of an accountability rating for districts that
  34| participated in on line, next generation MCAS testing. Lunenburg Middle School
  35| transitional student growth scores are well above the state average and indicate high
  36| student growth in five of the six content areas that have student growth measured.
  37| Although we are proud of our students’ performance, we continue to analyze data in our
  38| monthly results faculty meetings designed to improve our instruction for all students. Our

===PAGE 114=== (plain, 47 lines)
   1| 114
   2| faculty has identified areas of improvement based upon the data and implemented
   3| strategies to improve their instructional practices that will help students succeed.
   4| We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the
   5| needs of each individual student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built
   6| into our master schedule allow teachers time to work with students at their own
   7| individual skill level.  The philosophy is to challenge students who are high academic
   8| achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have identified weaknesses.
   9| Teachers are able to work with students in a small group to answer individual questions
  10| or provide more individualized support.  We design this support model to allow students
  11| to feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom.  We will continue to refine
  12| our intervention block times to best meet the needs of our students. One option for
  13| students during this intervention block is the use of our web based program called IXL.
  14| IXL allows students to work at their own skill level, build confidence and moved forward
  15| with work that is aligned with the state standards. IXL allows teachers a tool for true
  16| differentiation for every student allowing them to work and grow at their own pac e and
  17| level.
  18| Lunenburg Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students.
  19| Enrichment classes provide students a block of time in which they receive instruction in
  20| the areas of developmental health and guidance.   Students also receive in struction in
  21| physical education, art, and music classes that allows them to explore their strengths and
  22| talents. Students also have the opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and
  23| chorus during this block. This enrichment block is in addition to  the traditional special
  24| area classes students receive, and this block offers students additional physical movement
  25| throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on activities and more exposure to
  26| the arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
  27| We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different
  28| programs developed to promote social and emotional growth, self -awareness, and
  29| community service. We will continue to utilize Developmental Design program to deliver
  30| our social emotional curriculum. Students meet with teachers in small groups to teach
  31| social responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing empathy and promoting
  32| positive self -esteem and strong teacher/student relationships. Our social em otional
  33| programs have been developed to allow students to showcase their talents and abilities,
  34| grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger
  35| generation.
  36| This year, we have been able to offer foreign language exploratory t o our sixth and
  37| seventh grade students, giving them the opportunity to explore several different foreign
  38| language options in order to make an informed decision on which language they would
  39| like to take in grade eight and high school. The addition of the fo reign language
  40| instruction has been well received and the students enjoy exploring different cultures and
  41| languages.
  42| Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and
  43| supports that ensure student success and promotes a pas sion for lifelong learning. The
  44| key components to our success is our talented faculty committed to making a positive
  45| impact on students, our parents and community who support our  teaching and learning,
  46| and most of all, our students who inspire us through t heir commitment to learning.
  47| Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.

  17| Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2017 -2018.
  18| Teachers continue to provide our students rigorous coursework and hold our students to
  19| high standards.  Our students meet these challenges through collaborati on, self-advocacy,
  20| and strong work ethic.  All students in grades 10 -12 have been issued a Chrome Book
  21| and there are wireless capabilities throughout the building.  Every classroom and
  22| instructional space has an interactive projector and voice -lift capabil ities. Our students
  23| are fortunate to be in a building that can meet the needs of the 21st century learner.
  24| During the fall sports season, our students practiced and competed on our three grass
  25| fields and new artificial turf field.  The youth sports team s in t he community were
  26| scheduled on the fields, as well.  During the fall season, most of our fields were being
  27| used from 3:00 pm to 8:00 pm.

  29| for a large portion of the afternoon and evening by our basketball teams, indoor track and
  30| field teams, and students and community members in the Adult Education Program.
  31| Additionally, we have athletics teams practicing and competing at: Wallace Civic Center,
  32| The Groton School, Nashoba Valley Ski Area, and at Fitchburg High School.  Our actors
  33| and actresses have been rehearsing since January for the Competitive Class Plays.
  34| NEASC ACCREDITATION
  35| Lunenburg High School is currently going through the Self-Reflection portion of the
  36| accreditation process.  Teachers, students, and parents completed an online survey which
  37| has given the school feedback on the various standards that we need to measure.
  38| Teachers have collected evidence and will make claims as to where the school is in
  39| reference to the various principles within each standard.  The five standards are: Learning
  40| Culture, Student Learning, Professional Practices, Learning Support, and Learning
  41| Resources.  In May, two to four individuals from NEASC will come out to do a
  42| collaborative conference.  They will be presented with our self-reflection, along with
  43| recommendations and commendations.  The school will then develop and implement a

   2| growth plan over the next two years.  The final accreditation visit will be conducted in
   3| 2020 and will look at how well we are making progress on our growth plan.
   4| SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
   5| Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of
   6| fundraising and collection endeavors. In the fall, a canned goods drive was held and
   7| admission to the PowderPuff game was two canned goods.  Everything collected was
   8| donated to the Lunenburg Food Pantry.  Around the holiday season, advisories collected
   9| items for the Lunenburg Gift Mart.
  10| There is a Senior English class running t his year that has a community service learning
  11| component.  Students were tasked with researching and identifying needs in the
  12| community and then forming an action plan to address the needs.  For example, we have
  13| students working on: a pet shelter drive, cr eating trail maps for Lunenburg conservation
  14| land, bringing a small breakfast program to LHS, and forming a group to inform and
  15| educate about mental health issues.
  16| Lunenburg High School received a generous donation in the amount of $500 from Mr.
  17| Mike’s Mobil in Lunenburg, MA.  The grant is to be used toward STEM initiatives at the
  18| high school.  We are in the process of purchasing a new robot for our Robotics class.
  19| Hannaford Helps Schools made a generous donation to Lunenburg High School of $500
  20| from receipt sales.  This money will be used to benefit a variety of programs at LHS.
  21| In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive.  Students
  22| sixteen and over (and staff members) will be able to participate in the Blood Drive w ith
  23| parent permission.
  24| LHS has held evening presentations for parents/guardians on the following topics:
  25|  Planning for High School (grade 8)
  26|  Early College Planning (grades 9-10)
  27|  Post-Secondary Education Planning (grade 11)
  28|  College Application Process (grade 12)
  29|  Financing your Education/Financial Aid (grades 8-12)
  30| LHS hosted 40 college admissions counselors in the school counseling office to visit with
  31| LHS students.  In addition, we held two “Decision Day” programs with Fitchburg State
  32| University and Mount Wachusett Community College.
  33| MCAS RESULTS
  34| We are pleased to announce that Lunenburg High School is recognized by the
  35| Department of Elementary and Secondary Education as a Level 1 school.  As a Level 1
  36| school, we continue to be among the highest performing schools in the Commonwealth.
  37| We are very proud of the work that our students and teachers have done to prepare for the
  38| MCAS test.  While we are recognized as a high performing school, we understand this is
  39| only one measure used to show student growth and that there is constantly room for
  40| improvement

   1| 118
   2| Lunenburg High School - 2017 MCAS Results (Grade 10)
   3| Subject Test Advanced/Proficient State Average (Adv/Pro)
   4| ELA 93% 91%
   5| Math 90% 79%
   6| Science/Technology 84% 74%
   7| When you combine the percentage bands of the advanced and proficient range, LHS tenth
   8| grade students scored 93% on the ELA test.  In Mathematics, tenth grade students scored
   9| 90% advanced/proficient. In Science/Technology/Engineering, tenth grade students
  10| scored 84% advanced/proficient.  We are ex tremely proud of our students and teachers
  11| for their continued improvement, and we will work to move all students to the level of
  12| proficiency as measured by MCAS and our goal of college and career readiness as they
  13| move through our curriculum.
  14| We were al so very pleased to learn that 29 seniors at Lunenburg High School received
  15| the state’s John and Abigail Adams Scholarship this year.  The scholarship is available to
  16| students whose MCAS performance puts them at the top 25 percent of their district.  To
  17| be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other,
  18| or Advanced on both the English and Math exams.
  19| HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
  20| Homecoming was once again a great town -wide celebration, culminating with a beautiful
  21| parade complete with floats representing all four high school classes.  Many of our sports
  22| teams qualified for tournament and district play.  The Lunenburg High School Marching
  23| Band picked up second and third place at two competition s in the fall.  The LHS Mock
  24| Trial team has performed well this year, going 2 -1 in local trials.  The Latin Club, Jazz
  25| Band and the Math team represented LHS in numerous competitions; the 52nd annual
  26| Competitive Class Plays delighted the audience, and The  Pops and Senior Showcase are
  27| scheduled for the spring.
  28| COMMUNITY BUILDING
  29| National Honor Society successfully supported the “Giftmart” drive during the holidays,
  30| helping to provide joy to needy children and families in conjunction with local agencies.
  31| The World Hunger Task Force will be raising money through Penny Wars this spring.
  32| The money will be donated toward the fight against hunger.
  33| In March, the National Honor Society will be running its annual Blood Drive.  Students
  34| and staff participate in the drive.  Our NHS students are looking to add a second date and
  35| expand the blood drive for Lunenburg community members.
  36| We started an Advisory program two years ago.  One goal of Advisory is to have students
  37| have at least one adult who will get to know him/her over the course of four years.
  38| Students were randomly distributed into small grade-level groups.  They will stay with
  39| the same group of students, as well.   Another goal of Advisory is to develop
  40| relationships with students of different backgrounds and social groups so that students
  41| can get to know each other and develop an appreciation of the diversity that we do have
  42| in Lunenburg.  Some Advisories will be facilitating some community service learning
  43| projects.

===PAGE 122=== (plain, 46 lines)
   1| 122
   2| The 2016 -2017 school year marked my third year as Superintendent -Director of Montachusett  Regional
   3| Vocational Technical School District, and in my short tenure here, I am proud to say I have seen the school
   4| reach new levels of academic excellence, technical proficiency, and student engagement. Perhaps the most
   5| exciting aspect of my role here i s bearing witness to the creativity and innovation at work everyday in our
   6| classrooms and shops. Our students and instructors work collaboratively to explore scientific concepts,
   7| designing experiments that are conducted aboard the International Space Stati on; they study automation
   8| and additive manufacturing through the use of robotic arms and 3 -D printers; and even build new
   9| educational facilities that students will grow and learn in for years to come. Monty Tech is a school unlike
  10| any other and I am honored to serve the students, faculty and staff  here.
  11| In many respects, 2016 -2017 was a banner year at Monty Tech. We launched a whole school 1:1
  12| Chromebook initiative, providing all students with increased access to instructional technology, and
  13| improving communications between teachers and students beyond the traditional school day. We received
  14| more than $645,000 in competitive grants, and have been able to provide students with remarkable field
  15| trips to connect their learning to STEM career pathways that spa rk imagination and creativity.  We
  16| provided high quality professional development opportunities to support the school’s new biomedical
  17| courses, bringing innovative new science coursework and curriculum to students with an interest in
  18| biomedical and biotech nology career pathways. Construction of the Monty Tech Veterinary Science
  19| Training Center and Community Clinic continued throughout 2016 -2017, providing tremendous hands -on
  20| learning opportunities to students in our Carpentry, Plumbing, Electrical, Cabinetm aking, HVAC,
  21| Masonry, and Welding trades. School administrators launched an unprecedented fundraising campaign,
  22| and successfully raised more than $2.5M in donations and in -kind matching services to support this
  23| project. And finally, thanks to an extremel y generous Skills Capital Grant, we were able to renovate the
  24| Machine Technology shop, and purchase state -of-the-art machinery for students to train on, earning more
  25| industry recognized credentials than ever  before.
  26| We recognize that students attending Mo nty Tech have chosen a high school experience that, to many, may
  27| seem complex and even daunting. Our students are required to pass the same statewide standardized tests
  28| as students in a comprehensive high school. They take English, mathematics, science, s ocial studies and
  29| foreign language courses, just as high school students in your community might. A Monty Tech student,
  30| though, must also balance all of that with a rigorous vocational -technical education, specializing in one of
  31| our twenty programs. For fo ur years our students work and learn in vocational settings that replicate
  32| industry standards. They train on the latest equipment, and, through the school’s popular Co -operative
  33| Education Program, our students may even spend time working and learning in ar ea businesses during their
  34| Junior and Senior year. It is a great responsibility to provide each and every student with high -quality,
  35| vocational-technical instruction, and we are honored to serve approximately 1,400 students each year.
  36| This work is only po ssible because of our talented educators and administrators, who remain focused on
  37| delivering the highest quality academic and vocational -technical education possible. Working closely with
  38| post-secondary colleagues, we aim to ensure our students graduate p repared to enter college pathways of
  39| their choosing. Similarly, relying on our expansive network of industry partners, we strive to improve upon
  40| each of our vocational programs to ensure that every graduate is not only prepared for entry -level careers
  41| in related pathways, but are also creative, independent thinkers, capable of solving complex, real -world
  42| situations that present themselves in today’s workplaces. On their behalf, I am delighted to present the
  43| District’s 2016-2017 annual report to you, provid ing a snapshot of the wonderful experiences happening on
  44| a day-to-day basis here at Monty Tech.
  45| Respectfully submitted,
  46| Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D.,  Superintendent -Director
