   2| Our Mission
   3| Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills,
   4| knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever -changing society.
   5| Our District
   6| Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four-year career and technical high school
   7| serving the member towns of:
   8| Ashburnham
   9| Ashby
  10| Athol
  11| Barre
  12| Fitchburg
  13| Gardner
  14| Harvard
  15| Holden
  16| Hubbardston
  17| Lunenburg
  18| Petersham
  19| Phillipston
  20| Princeton
  21| Royalston
  22| Sterling
  23| Templeton
  24| Westminster
  25| Winchendon
  26| Leadership
  27| The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented
  28| administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise
  29| contribute to t he success of the school. Working collaboratively, and under the direction of the
  30| Superintendent and Principal, the team has transformed the school into one of the most sought - after high
  31| schools in North Central Massachusetts.
  32| Sheila M. Harrity, Superintendent-Director
  33| Tom Browne, Principal
  34| Dayana Carlson, Assistant Principal
  35| Tammy Crockett, Business Manager
  36| Pamela Pothier, Director of Technology
  37| Christina Favreau, Director of Academic Programs
  38| Jim Hachey, Director of Vocational Programs
  39| Michael Gormley, Director of Facilities
  40| Katy Whitaker, Development Coordinator
  41| Victoria Zarozinski, Director of Student Support Services
  42| Enrollment
  43| On June 1, 2017, student enrollment at Monty Tech
  44| included 1,403 students in grades nine through twelve,
  45| representing each of the district’s eighteen sending
  46| communities. Each class of students is comprised of a
  47| relatively equal balance of male vs. female students,
  48| and each student attending Monty Tech has elected to
  49| do so, prepared to explore an interest in one of t he
  50| school’s twenty rigorous vocational -technical
  51| programs. While the school currently offers only five
  52| programs non -traditional for male students, and
  53| twelve programs non - traditional for female students,
  54| we are proud to have a student population that is well-
  55| balanced by gender and a variety of academic interests
  56| and achievements.

===PAGE 124=== (plain, 31 lines)
   1| 124
   2| Throughout 2016-2017, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and
   3| community members to learn about and visit the school. In October 2016, approximately 700 dis-
   4| trict  eighth graders participated in the annual “Tour Day” event. Students toured our twenty voca-
   5| tional- technical areas and learned about the school’s challenging academic offerings and exciting
   6| athletic and extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the op-
   7| portunity to return in the evening with their family members to further explore the facilities and
   8| speak with staff members.
   9| The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance
  10| to visit Monty Tech after school and participate in hands -on learning experiences across a variety
  11| of vocational/technical areas. The program continued to attract a large number of students during
  12| the 2016- 2017 school year, serving approximately 600 area students.
  13| Class of 2017 Awards
  14| Members of the Class of 2017 were awarded approximately $88,950 in scholarships. The Monty
  15| Tech Foundation generously provided $45,700 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in
  16| amounts of $200 to $2,000. The Foundation also awarded $4,000 to the Practical Nursing gradu-
  17| ates. Once again, local and  state organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recog-
  18| nize the ability and potential of  Monty Tech graduates in the form of financial donations. The
  19| School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves, are grateful for this  support.
  20| Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost
  21| of  higher education. Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through
  22| a number  of articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country. Approxi-
  23| mately 51% of the graduating class of 2017 reported plans to enroll at a 4 -year college/university
  24| upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will save both
  25| time and money as they pursue advanced educational  programs.
  26| Financial Report
  27| A great deal of effort was put forth by the School Committee, administration, and staff to develop a
  28| cost- effective budget for the fiscal year 2016 -2017. The final fiscal year 2016 -2017 Educational
  29| Plan totaled $26,374,468, which represents a .6% increase over the 2015 -2016 Educational Plan.
  30| The District’s FY17 budget  exceeds  the  minimum  spending  required  by  Massachusetts  General
  31| Law  Chapter  70  by only $45,000 or .2%.

===PAGE 125=== (plain, 27 lines)
   1| 125
   2| The District was audited in October 2017 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of
   3| Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA,  and received a very good report.
   4| Grants and Contracts
   5| Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds help provide many
   6| educational and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2017, state and federal grant
   7| sources provided the school with $961,346. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health
   8| Services, Social Intervention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and
   9| Program Improvement, Title I Support, Perkins Occup ational Education, and Marine Corp Junior Reserve
  10| Officer Training. The District also received more than $645,000 in competitive grants, including a Skills
  11| Capital Grant for $435,857 to purchase equipment for the Machine Technology Program, and a grant fro m
  12| the Massachusetts Life Sciences Center in the amount of $99,697 to expand the school’s science program
  13| to include biomedical coursework and learning laboratories. Using these allocation and competitive funds,
  14| the school was able to purchase a variety of instructional technology, equipment, and supplies to enhance
  15| the learning experience across both academic and vocational programs.
  16| Academic Achievement
  17| In 2016-2017, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to demonstrate
  18| high academic achievement, earning commendable MCAS scores in English Language Arts, Mathematics
  19| and Biology. For Spring 2017, Monty Tech’s passing rate on the English Language Arts was 100%,
  20| Mathematics 97%, Biology 98%.
  21| Monty Tech is committed to provi ding students with rigorous STEM learning opportunities, and as such,
  22| has expanded the Advanced Placement Program to include two mathematics, two science, and one
  23| computer science course. Teacher participation in meaningful, high -quality professional devel opment in
  24| AP and Pre -AP pedagogy remains a priority, and enables us to continually expand and enhance our
  25| academic course offerings.
  26| The school is in its 5th year of administering Advanced Placement exams, and students enrolled in AP
  27| courses continue to improve their performance. In 2016 -2017, Monty Tech had more students achieve

  18| Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  19| In addition to applying their academic skills, students at Monty Tech are a sked to put their vocational -
  20| technical skills into practice on a daily basis. Students across the twenty different vocational -technical
  21| education programs are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles,
  22| making walkways more accessible, and performing countless community services.
  23| The 2016-2017 school year was a busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide
  24| practical, hands -on learning experiences for our students, while helping to improve each of t he eighteen
  25| member communities.
  26| Auto Body Collision Repair Technology : The Monty Tech Auto Body shop benefitted from new
  27| instructional equipment this year, including a new Frame Machine. In addition, 2016 -2017 marked the first
  28| year for graduating Seniors to receive the I -Car On -Line Pro Level One certification in refinishing and
  29| non- structural damage. This certification not only makes our program graduates more employable, it
  30| enables students to maintain the I -CAR gold class certification. Seniors also r eceived an EPA 6H
  31| Certificate, while the Freshmen completed their Career Safe Online OSHA 10 -hour General Industry
  32| Training. Throughout the year, students detailed, repaired, and repainted vehicles, fulfilling more than
  33| seventy requests that included: a tr ailer for the Fitchburg Police Department, and a van for the Worcester
  34| Fire Brigade. A Senior student won a gold Medal at the SkillsUSA state competition in the collision repair
  35| category, and qualified for the National event in June 2017. A total of eight students (four Seniors and four
  36| Juniors) participated in the school’s popular Co -op Program, working in area auto body shops and
  37| businesses. (Total enrollment: 65; 40 males, 25 females)
  38| Automotive Technology: As in past years, the Monty Tech Automotive Te chnology program continues to
  39| service a variety of vehicles for faculty, staff, municipalities, and district residents . 2016-2017 proved to be
  40| a very busy year for the program, as more than four hundred vehicles were serviced by students and

===PAGE 127=== (plain, 48 lines)
   1| 127
   2| instructors. In addition to maintaining the school’s fleet of vehicles throughout the year, students were asked
   3| to repair a Ford Excursion for the Bulldog Pride giveaway. Mr. Hobbs worked with a group of students to
   4| refurbish a 1966 Pontiac 2+2 so that so it could be auctioned off. Students performed very well in the
   5| SkillsUSA district competitions, placing first, second, and third, with hopes to improve in the  coming year. A
   6| total of eight Senior and four Junior students participated in the Co -op Program, applying their technical skills
   7| in a real work setting. Finally, instructors are proud to announce that three talented program graduates are
   8| working in automotive repair shops in the area, demonstrating the skills they acquired at Monty Tech. (Total
   9| enrollment: 64; 50 males, 14 females)
  10| Business Technology: Students in Monty Tech’s Business Technology program continue to benefit from a
  11| unique partnership with Workers’ Credit Union. The opportunity to participate in teller training and financial
  12| literacy workshops has been met with great enthusiasm from our students, and the instructors continue to be
  13| grateful for this developing partnership. Monty Tech Business Technology instructors, in collaboration with
  14| their post -secondary colleagues, continue to refine and develop new articulation agreements. These agree-
  15| ments, with Mount Wachusett Community College and New England Institute of Technology, will provide
  16| qualified students with college credits at no cost to the student. Opportunities to demonstrate customer ser-
  17| vice, cash handling, and accounting skills are ever -present as the Business Technology students successfully
  18| operate an in -house retail location and greenhouse. The Gear House School Store acquired a credit card ma-
  19| chine, which has made a significant impact on the students’ retail  and customer service experience. In addi-
  20| tion, the camera in the Gear House was replaced, providing the instructors with improved vision on store ac-
  21| tivities while students are working. Eight Seniors and three Juniors were out on Co -op placements during the
  22| 2016-2017 school year. (Total enrollment: 70; 20 males, 50 females)
  23| Cabinetmaking: All Monty Tech vocational instructors strive to provide Freshman exploratory programs that
  24| not only represent the work and skills students can expect to encounter throughout their high school experi-
  25| ence, but also one that piques the interest of students new to the trade. Monty Tech Cabinetmaking instructors
  26| do an outstanding job with the Freshmen exploratory experience, and in 2016 -2017,  the program reached
  27| capacity with students who listed Cabinetmaking as their top choice. Throughout the year, Cabinetmaking
  28| students designed and manufactured approximately 400 gifts for the Superintendent's dinner, manufactured
  29| and assembled seventy-four cabinet assemblies for the new Veterinary Science Training Center, and complet-
  30| ed more than thirty -five additional projects in and around the school, to include: Student Services conference
  31| room, Chromebook mobile charging stations, trophy Case for the Gardner Skating Rink, new signage for the
  32| Barre Cemetery, new lettering for the Ashby Fire Department, surfaced planed two large tree butts for the
  33| Mount Wachusett Community College, and play sets for Crocker Elementary School. Finally, Cabinetmaking
  34| had another great year of Co -op placement and continues to receive calls from area businesses in need of
  35| qualified employees. A total of fifteen students (seven Seniors and eight Juniors) were placed in Co -op expe-
  36| riences, and an additional two students earned full -time employment over the summer. (Total enrollment: 70;
  37| 50 males, 20 females)
  38| Cosmetology: With a significant increase in the number of clients served this year, the Monty Tech Cos-
  39| metology program saw a significant revenue increase, which will be used to fund the program’s much - need-
  40| ed consumable products. The new Salon Iris point-of-sale system was installed, which is a more user- friendly
  41| and cost effective program for students to train on. In an effort to support the school’s  commitment to com-
  42| munity service, the Junior students traveled to Heywood Wakefield Assisted Living Center on several occa-
  43| sions, providing residents with manicures, styling hair for a Senior Citizen Prom, and offered discounted ser-
  44| vices on “Staff Night” to fundraise for the field trip to New York City. Cosmetology students also provided
  45| manicure and hair services for the school’s Child Care Center, serviced parents from Athol that work with
  46| disadvantaged children, and assisted our Drama club with hair and makeup. The program’s greatest accom-
  47| plishment was realized when 100% of the Senior class passed the Massachusetts Cosmetology State Boards,
  48| earning licenses to practice. (Total enrollment: 91, 0 males, 91 females)

===PAGE 128=== (layout, 48 lines)
   1|    Culinary Arts: Monty Tech Culinary Arts students and instructors are always busy with the daily
   2|    operation of the Mountain Room Restaurant, which is open for lunch from 11:30am to 1:00pm
   3|    Monday through Friday. In addition to operating a full-service restaurant and bakery, serving 90-
   4|    120 patrons daily, culinary students showcase their talents throughout the year, providing outstand-

  17|                                      Co-operative Education and Student Placement
  18|    The Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student’s technical education that com-
  19|    bines classroom instruction with on-the-job-training. The Co-op Program provides students the op-
  20|    portunity to further develop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry work envi-
  21|    ronment. All students are eligible to participate in Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and
  22|    school grade, attendance and performance requirements. All Co-op students have completed the-
  23|    OSHA recognized Career Safe online health and safety course.
  24|    The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer. For the student, it
  25|    is a chance to gain on-the job training that improves both their technical and employability skills.
  26|    Additionally, making industry connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities. The
  27|    Co-op Program allows students to earn while they learn.
  28|    Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner. Coop students pro-
  29|    vide a pool of temporary and potential full-time employees who are already trained, thus reducing
  30|    employer training costs. Co-op work hours may be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employ-
  31|    ers. During school year 2016-2017, a record number of students participated in the cooperative ed-
  32|    ucation   program.     Approximately       37%    of  the  class  of  2017   earned    Co-op    placements,     working,
  33|    learning and applying their technical skills related in area businesses.

===PAGE 133=== (plain, 30 lines)
   1| 133
   2| Student Support Services
   3| During the 2016-2017 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District pro-
   4| vided special services to approximately 300 students – measuring progress of over 200 students on
   5| Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and just under 100 students adhering to individualized Section 504
   6| plans. While the Student Support Services (SSS) Dept. encompasses special education, the depart-
   7| ment provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
   8| The department includes a full -time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state -
   9| mandated health screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health information to the special
  10| education team for a student’s IEP meeting. The department benefits from a full -time school social
  11| worker that participates in departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning
  12| finances, family issues, homelessness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. The
  13| school is also fortunate to have on staff a full -time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all stu-
  14| dents referred for an initial evaluation or who require a three - year re-evaluation. In addition, we
  15| have a full-time speech language pathologist, who is available to assist students with disabilities, as-
  16| sess these students and consult with teachers. Our students also have access to the services of a full -
  17| time adjustment counselor and part -time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available
  18| for scheduled counseling sessions, mental health emergency treatment, medical/mental health re -
  19| entry and transition support and services, as well as crisis intervention.
  20| The school’s Director of Student Support Services oversees the District’s Special Education Pro-
  21| gram, which is reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The compre-
  22| hensive  review and evaluation are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the
  23| results of the evaluation are used to improve the special education procedures and programs in place
  24| at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School.
  25| Technology
  26| The 2016-2017 school year began with a successful roll -out of the long anticipated 1:1 Chromebook
  27| initiative. Chromebooks were distributed to 1,436 students. Google cloud Print was added, making
  28| printers available to students and teachers throughout the building. Two Chromebook charging sta-
  29| tions were installed in the library and cafeteria allowing students to charge their devices, and stu-
  30| dents who may have come to school without their devices have been allowed to borrow a

===PAGE 134=== (layout, 43 lines)
   1|   Chromebook from the library, on a daily basis.
   2|   To support the 1:1 Chromebook initiative and other technology needs, Monty Tech has successfully
   3|   completed  a  number  of  necessary  infrastructure  upgrades.  With  federal  e-rate  dollars  totaling
   4|   $77,752, the school’s external bandwidth speeds were increased to 1.5GB and internal bandwidth
   5|   was increased by 10GB. In addition to hardware, the school added the following complementary
   6|   software: Lightspeed web filtering and Aruba ClearPass. Lightspeed web filtering is expected to
   7|   provide safe, fast access to the Internet and includes flexible policies, social media controls, band-
   8|   width control, malware protection, anonymous proxy detection, mobile filtering, classroom man-
   9|   agement tools, and comprehensive reporting. Aruba ClearPass will provide strong network access
  10|   security for monitoring wireless devices, as well as enhanced options for guest access services.
  11|   The school also launched a Digital Citizenship course, proving lessons and guidance relating to ap-
  12|   propriate online behavior, including cyberbullying, potential risks related to social networking sites
  13|   and chat rooms. Students at Monty Tech are expected to demonstrate the safe and responsible use
  14|   of technology and an understanding of security, privacy, and ethics. The district maintains Internet
  15|   Safety information on the district website in the Parents & Students section.

  25|     program, by qualifying for the District tournament.
  26|     The Varsity Baseball team had a very successful season, despite not playing any games at Monty Tech. They
  27|     finished the season with a 14 – 7 record, winning the Colonial Athletic League small school Championship
  28|     for the second straight year. The JV Baseball team finished 10 – 4, while the Freshmen team continued to
  29|     provide build skills and talent for the program’s future. The Varsity Softball team had a great regular season
  30|     as they finished at 17 – 3. The team lost to Blackstone Valley Tech 15 – 14 in the Semi- Finals of the State
  31|     Vocational Tournament and lost to Quaboag with a score of 4 – 0 in the first round of the District tourna-
  32|     ment. The JV Softball team, comprised primarily of freshmen also had an excellent season, finishing with a
  33|     record of 12 – 3. The Boys and Girls Track and Field teams, with almost fifty students participating, both
  34|     finished their seasons with 6 - 4 records. The Varsity Boys Lacrosse team had their best season ever as they
  35|     finished with a record of 14 – 6 in the regular season, losing to Blackstone Valley Tech 14 – 12 in the semi-
  36|     final round of the State Vocational Tournament. The team then outscored Blackstone Valley 12 – 8 in the
  37|     first round of the MIAA tournament, only to lose to Grafton in the next round. The JV Boys Lacrosse team
  38|     finished their building year with a record of 6 – 10. The Girls Lacrosse team became was the host team in a
  39|     Co -Op with Sizer School and continued to improve as they finished                      3–16, but third place in the Colonial
  40|     Athletic League. The Varsity Boys Volleyball team was 13 – 7, qualifying for the MIAA tournament, but
  41|     losing to Worcester Technical High School in the first round.
  42|     Congratulations to the Outstanding Male and Female athletes for 2016-2017, Travis Hull and Madison Rus-
  43|     so. Dave Reid, Monty Tech’s long-time Athletic Director, completed his forty-fourth year as Varsity Softball
  44|     coach, and was selected as the Section 1 Varsity Softball Coach of the Year. His overall record of 626 – 315
  45|     – 1 speaks to his tremendous contributions to the sport and sets him apart from other coaches in Section 1,
  46|     which is includes all of Maine, New Hampshire, Vermont, Massachusetts, Rhode Island, Connecticut, New
  47|     Jersey and New York.

===PAGE 136=== (layout, 45 lines)
   1|                                          Monty Tech School of Continuing Education
   2|    The Monty Tech School of Continuing Education continues to update and add courses that empha-
   3|    size a commitment to excellence by offering affordable, quality, and enjoyable educational experi-
   4|    ences. For the Fall of 2016, Monty Tech offered 65 classes with 580 registrations, and during the
   5|    Spring 2017 semester, there were 79 postgraduate and continuing studies courses, with 857 regis-
   6|    trations.
   7|    The program is on track to expand the number of workforce readiness classes that provide our stu-
   8|    dents with industry-recognized credentials, increasing our small business partnerships, and the
   9|    number and quality of personal enrichment classes - sewing, knitting, acrylic painting and sculpt-
  10|    ing, to name a few. Catalog distribution has almost doubled, from 80,000 to 155,000 copies, and
  11|    the relatively new online registration feature continues to add value to our students and instructors.
  12|    Spring 2018 will see the program’s first Cosmetology courses come to fruition. This will provide
  13|    our community members with a high-quality, affordable entry into a viable career pathway. Upon
  14|    completion of the 14-month program, students will have the opportunity to sit for the cosmetology
  15|    licensing exam, and begin their work in the exciting cosmetology industry.
  16|    The continued success and sustainability of an adult education program is based on hiring quality
  17|    instructors and meeting the needs of the community. Our goal in the coming year includes increas-
  18|    ing our adult education course offerings, with a concentration on career programs. Stay tuned for
  19|    Pharmacy Technician, Welding Certification Prep, Auto Damage Appraiser and Apprenticeship
  20|    opportunities at the Monty Tech School of Continuing Education.
  21|                                                 Practical Nursing Program
  22|   The Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in a
  23|    caring manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in diverse
  24|    health care  settings. This mission which is consistent with the philosophy and goals of the Monta-
  25|    chusett Regional Vocational Technical School District, accomplishes the following:
  26|       Identifies a strong relationship between academic and vocational preparation
  27|       Stresses the importance of developing critical thinking skills to function safely, effec-
  28|        tively, and productively in an ever-changing technical and diverse society
  29|       Supports the maintenance of a positive and caring learning and practice environment
  30|   The above mission and philosophy were met by the Practical Nursing program by several different
  31|    methods.
  32|   On June 29, 2017, a graduating class of 38 students completed the Practical Nursing Program and
  33|    entered the nursing profession. The class achieved an initial NCLEX pass rate of 76%, with 28 of
  34|    the 38 graduates achieving 100% pass rate for the NCLEX-PN exam. Seven graduates are waiting
  35|    to retake their NCLEX-          PN exam, and two graduates have already taken the exam again,                        and
  36|    passed.
  37|    78% of the 2017 graduates  are currently employed in the health care profession throughout Mas-
  38|    sachusetts, and many are working within the eighteen cities and towns of the Monty Tech school
  39|    district as LPNs in various health care settings, ranging from long term care, sub-acute care to
  40|    mental health facilities.
  41|    78% of the 2017 graduates  are currently employed in the health care profession throughout Mas-
  42|    sachusetts, and many are working within the eighteen cities and towns of the Monty Tech school
  43|    district as LPNs in various health care settings, ranging from long term care, sub-acute care to
  44|    mental health facilities.

===PAGE 137=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 137
   2| The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to develop the “LPN to BSN Bridge” relation-
   3| ship with Fitchburg State University. Several 2017 graduates are pursuing seats in the LPN to BSN
   4| program at FSU and will be continuing their education to the Bachelors in Nursing. Many class appli-
   5| cants cited their attraction to Monty Tech as the opportunity to complete a ten -month accelerated pro-
   6| gram and then bridge to Fitchburg State University.
   7| Monty Tech Practical Nursing students are now completing patient scenarios in the Sim Lab on a
   8| weekly basis in Terms 2 and 3. The Faculty Sim team has implemented National League of Nursing
   9| (NLN) patient scenarios consistent with our curriculum frameworks, and have also utilized the Sim
  10| lab setting to instruct students in developing nursing skills that they may not be experiencing in the
  11| clinical setting. Instructors have successfully developed a pediatric patient scenario that is consistent
  12| with present student learning outcomes and the clinical pediatric setting. Similarly, instructors have
  13| implemented a scenario that deals with a mental health addiction client, so that our students are better
  14| prepared for the clinical experience at partnering institution AdCare Hospital in Worcester, MA.
  15| The class of 2017 performed a community service project adopting a Monty Tech family in need re-
  16| ferred by Student Support Services. The nursing students purchased holiday gifts and food, consistent
  17| with the family’s needs and requests.
  18| The Practical Nursing Program Faculty spent the summer of 2017 reviewing the revised 2017 NCLEX
  19| -PN Detailed Test Plan, and the program’s student outcomes, to assure alignment and compliance
  20| with our curriculum frameworks and content. The annual review process is an important component
  21| of the program, ensuring the Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program presents students with rigorous
  22| content, aligned with industry standards, so that our graduates are prepared to succeed not only on the
  23| licensing exam, but also in their chosen nursing career pathway.
  24| With an eye toward the program’s future, fifty -one applicants were accepted to the Class of 2018, and
  25| forty students enrolled and will continue in the tradition of high -quality practical nursing preparation.
  26| Looking Ahead
  27| While the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District educational community is cer-
  28| tainly proud of the achievements of our talented students, faculty, and staff, we continue to have an
  29| eye toward the future, always committed to improving our vocational and academic programming,
  30| strengthening key partnerships, and maintaining facilities that contribute to student success and
  31| achievement. As we look ahead, there are a number of programs and initiatives that we expect will
  32| have a positive impact on our school and students for years to come.
  33| Expand science offerings to include new biomedical courses : In 2016-2017 the school received a
  34| $99,000 grant from the Massachusetts Life Science Center, which allowed us to purchase new instruc-
  35| tional materials and equipment, provide the required, high quality professional development needed to
  36| teach new coursework, and renovate an existing science laboratory to replicate biomedical industry
  37| standards. Dylan Hager, a longtime science instructor at Monty Tech traveled to University of San
  38| Diego July of 2017, and will return July of 2018, to complete training in two Project Lead the Way
  39| courses, Principles of Biomedical Science and Human Body Systems.
  40| These courses, which will be available to students Fall 2018, are expected to engage students in a vari-
  41| ety of hands-on learning opportunities in a newly renovated laboratory environment that replicates
  42| industry standards. Students will be trained in the safe and effective use of the same tools that profes-
  43| sionals in hospitals and laboratories are currently using and will work together to find solutions to real
  44| -world problems by analyzing medical histories, reading and interpreting autopsy reports, and under-
  45| standing basic biology and human physiology.

===PAGE 138=== (layout, 31 lines)
   1|    Expand vocational-technical programs to include new, Chapter 74-approved, Veterinary Science
   2|    program: While construction and fundraising efforts for the school’s new program have been un-
   3|    derway for almost two years, to effectively bring a new program to a Massachusetts vocational-
   4|    technical school, a comprehensive application and supporting documents must be accepted by the
   5|    Department of Elementary and Secondary Education. During the 2017-2018 school year, school
   6|    officials will complete the  application, finalize all requirements outlined in the application, and
   7|    anticipate being granted approval for the new secondary-level program titled Animal Science.
   8|    This approval is given pursuant to Massachusetts General Law Chapter 74 and the Vocational
   9|    Technical Education Regulations 603 CMR 4.00, and will enable all interested Freshman to ex-
  10|    plore the new program. A Freshman class (class size of twenty-two students) will be accepted into
  11|    the program January 2018.
  12|    Expand post-secondary and continuing education opportunities: As you know, Montachusett Re-
  13|    gional Vocational Technical School is home to a very popular evening program that includes a
  14|    postsecondary Practical Nursing program, Plumbing and Electrical apprenticeship programs, and
  15|    continuing education courses that range from healthcare to advanced manufacturing to cake deco-
  16|    rating. While we are proud of our current evening programs, our goal for the 2017-2018 school
  17|    year is to expand programs to include an entirely electronic registration and payment process, an
  18|    updated web presence, increased enrollment, and a minimum of three new workforce develop-
  19|    ment programs that result in an industry recognized credential or certification. Catalogs highlight-
  20|    ing each new course will be mailed to more than 180,000 households bi- annually, in August and
  21|    January.
  22|    Redesign the school’s website and expand marketing efforts: A Monty Tech high school experi-
  23|    ence is an educational pathway students must choose, and to ensure that every interested student
  24|    in our sending district has access to information needed to make sound decisions regarding his/her
  25|    high school  experience, it is imperative that the school’s marketing efforts are expanded to in-
  26|    clude a newly designed website, with descriptions of each vocational shop, academic programs,
  27|    and admissions criteria. In addition, expanding our marketing efforts to include a social media
  28|    presence that is current and engaging  is a top priority. During the 2017-2018 school year, school
  29|    officials anticipate launching a new school website, adding updated content to our Facebook page
  30|    on a regular basis, and launching a school/district Twitter account.
  31|                                                               138

===PAGE 146=== (plain, 28 lines)
   1| 146
   2|  DETERMINATION OF ASSESSMENT RATIOS
   3| Each Community's assessment is made up of four parts:
   4| ~ Required Minimum Contribution (set by the State)
   5| ~ Transportation & Other Operating Expenses above Minimum Net School Spending
   6| ~ Capital Outlay
   7| ~ Bonds (assessed based upon the Capital apportionment)
   8| TRANSPORTATION & OTHER OPERATING BUDGET - (determined by each Communities enrollment, October 1, 2017)
   9| Formula:
  10| The number of students from each member community enrolled at Montachusett R egional Vocational Technical School
  11| divided by total Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School Day school enrollment of member communities
  12| on October 1, 2017 equals the operating ratio.
  13| Example:
  14| Fitchburg students enrolled at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School on October 1, 2017 = 398.
  15| Total Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School enrollment of 18 member communities on October 1, 2014 = 1,496.
  16| Therefore, ratio is 398 divided by 1,496.
  17| 398
  18|  1496 = 0.2660 = 26.60%
  19| CAPITAL BUDGET - (determined by each Communities school attending children, grades 1 - 12, October 1, 2017)
  20| Formula:
  21| The number of students from each member community enrolled in Grades 1 through 12 divided by the total number of
  22| students enrolled in Grades 1 through 12 of 18 member communities equals the capital assessment ratio.
  23| Example:
  24| Holden students enrolled grades 1 through 12 on October 1, 2017 = 2,672.
  25|  Total enrollment of 18 member communities in Grades 1 through 12 on October 1, 2014 = 24,147.
  26|  Therefore, ratio is 2,672 divided by 24,147.
  27| 3059
  28|  24711 = 0.1238 = 12.38%

  17|                          party has not filed in good faith a pending application for an abatement of such tax or a pending petition
  18|                          before the appellate tax board.
  19|                            (b) The licensing authority may deny, revoke or suspend any license or permit, including renewals and
  20|                          transfers of any party whose name appears on said list furnished to the licensing authority from the tax
  21|                          collector or with respect to any activity, event or other matter which is the subject of such license or
  22|                          permit and which activity, event or matter is carried out or exercised or is to be carried out or
  23|                          exercised on or about real estate owned by any party whose name appears on said list furnished to
  24|                          the licensing authority from the tax collector; provided, however, that written notice is given to the
  25|                          party and the tax collector, as required by applicable provisions of law, and the party is given a hearing, to
  26|                          be held not earlier than fourteen days after said notice. Said list shall be prima facie evidence for denial,
  27|                          revocation or suspension of said license or permit to any party. The tax collector shall have the right to
  28|                          intervene in any hearing conducted with respect to such license denial, revocation or suspension. Any
  29|                          findings made by the licensing authority with respect to such denial, revocation or suspension shall be
  30|                          made only for the purposes of such proceeding and shall not be relevant to or introduced in any other
  31|                          proceeding at law, except for any appeal from such license denial, revocation or suspension. Any license
  32|                          or permit denied, suspended or revoked under this section shall not be reissued or renewed until the
  33|                          licensing authority receives a certificate issued by the tax collector that the party is in good standing with
  34|                          respect to any and all local taxes, fees, assessments, betterments or other municipal charges payable to the
  35|                          Town as of the date of issuance of said certificate.; or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted
  36|                          by the Treasurer/Tax Collector) Board of Selectmen recommends approval. Finance Committee - no
  37|                          recommendation. VOTED to amend the General Bylaws of the Town by amending Article II

  13|                        ARTICLE 33. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw, to add Section 4.18 Temporary
  14|                        Moratorium on the Sale and Distribution of Recreational Marijuana and further to amend the Table of
  15|                        Contents to insert Section 4.18 Temporary Moratorium; or take any action relative thereto. Board of
  16|                        Selectmen recommends approval. Finance Committee reports no direct financial impact. VOTED
  17|                        UNANIMOUSLY to insert a new Section 4.18, Temporary Moratorium on the Sale and
  18|                        Distribution of Recreational Marijuana and to amend the Table of Contents by adding the words
  19|                       "Temporary Moratorium on the Sale and Distribution of Recreational Marijuana" as follows:
  20|                        4.18 Temporary Moratorium on the Sale and Distribution of Recreational Marijuana.
  21|                        4.18.1 Purpose:
  22|                                   By vote at the State election on November 8, 2016, the voters of the Commonwealth approved a law
  23|                                  regulating the cultivation, distribution, possession and use of marijuana for recreational purposes.
  24|                                   The law provides that it is effective on December 15, 2016 and the Cannabis Control Commission
  25|                                  is currently required to issue regulations regarding implementation by March 15, 2018 (see Section
  26|                                   11 of Chapter 351 of the Acts of 2016).
  27|                                   Currently under the Zoning Bylaw, Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana
  28|                                   Retailers are not a permitted use in the Town and any regulations promulgated by the State
  29|                                   Cannabis Control Commission are expected to provide guidance to the Town in regulating
  30|                                   Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana Retailers.
  31|                                   The regulation of Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana Retailers raise novel and
  32|                                   complex legal, planning, and public safety issues and the Town needs time to study and consider
  33|                                  the regulation of Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana Retailers and address such
  34|                                   novel and complex issues, as well as to address the potential impact of the State regulations on
  35|                                   local zoning and to undertake a planning process to consider amending the Zoning Bylaw
  36|                                  regarding regulation of Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana Retailers and other
  37|                                   uses related to the regulation of recreational marijuana. The Town intends to adopt a temporary
  38|                                   moratorium on the use of land and structures in the Town for Recreational Marijuana
  39|                                   Establishments and Marijuana Retailers so as to allow the Town sufficient time to engage in a
  40|                                   planning process to address effects of such structures and uses in the Town and to adopt
  41|                                                                                              164

===PAGE 165=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                                       provisions of the Zoning Bylaw in a manner consistent with sound land use planning goals and
   2|                                       objectives.
   3|                            4.18.2 Definitions.
   4|                                       "Manufacture", to compound, blend, extract, infuse or otherwise make or prepare a marijuana
   5|                                       product.
   6|                                       "Marijuana accessories", equipment, products, devices or materials of any kind that are intended or
   7|                                       designed for use in planting, propagating, cultivating, growing, harvesting, manufacturing,
   8|                                       compounding, converting, producing, processing, preparing, testing, analyzing, packaging,
   9|                                       repackaging, storing, containing, ingesting, inhaling or otherwise introducing marijuana into the
  10|                                       human body.
  11|                                       "Marijuana cultivator", an entity licensed to cultivate, process and package marijuana, to
  12|                                       deliver marijuana to marijuana establishments and to transfer marijuana to other marijuana
  13|                                       establishments, but not to consumers.
  14|                                       "Marijuana establishment", a marijuana cultivator, marijuana testing facility, marijuana product
  15|                                       manufacturer, marijuana retailer or any other type of licensed marijuana-related business.
  16|                                       "Marijuana product manufacturer", an entity licensed to obtain, manufacture, process and package
  17|                                       marijuana and marijuana products, to deliver marijuana and marijuana products to marijuana
  18|                                       establishments and to transfer marijuana and marijuana products to other marijuana establishments,
  19|                                       but not to consumers.
  20|                                       "Marijuana products", products that have been manufactured and contain marijuana or an extract
  21|                                        rom marijuana, including concentrated forms of marijuana and products composed of marijuan
  22|                                        und other ingredients that are intended for use or consumption, including edible products, beverages
  23|                                       topical products, ointments, oils and tinctures.
  24|                                       "Marijuana testing facility", an entity licensed to test marijuana and marijuana products,
  25|                                       including certification for potency and the presence of contaminants.
  26|                                       "Marijuana retailer", an entity licensed to purchase and deliver marijuana and marijuana
  27|                                       products from marijuana establishments and to deliver, sell or otherwise transfer marijuana and
  28|                                       marijuana products to marijuana establishments and to consumers.
  29|                            4.18.3 Temporary Moratorium.
  30|                                       For the reasons set forth above and notwithstanding any other provision of the Zoning Bylaw to
  31|                                       the contrary, the Town hereby adopts a temporary moratorium on the use of land or structures for
  32|                                       Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana Retailers. The moratorium shall be in
  33|                                       effect through November 30, 2018. During the moratorium period, the Town shall undertake a
  34|                                       planning process to address the potential impacts of recreational marijuana in the Town,
  35|                                       consider the Cannabis Control Commission regulations regarding Recreational Marijuana
  36|                                       Establishments and Marijuana Retailers and related uses, , determine whether the town will
  37|                                       prohibit on-site consumption at Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana Retailers
  38|                                       and shall consider adopting new provisions of the Zoning Bylaw to address the impact and
  39|                                       operation of Recreational Marijuana Establishments and Marijuana Retailers and related uses.
  40|                                                                                              165

===PAGE 166=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                               4.18.4 Severability.
   2|                                          The provisions of this Bylaw are severable. If any provision, paragraph, sentence, or clause of this
   3|                                          Bylaw or the application thereof to any person, establishment, or circumstances shall be held
   4|                                          invalid
